\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.

For any path
\mathbf{p}=\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{k}\right)
and any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, we set

\displaystyle A_{\ell}^{\mathbf{p}}

\displaystyle:=A_{\ell}^{v_{0}}A_{\ell}^{v_{1}}\cdots A_{\ell}^{v_{k}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{\mathbf{p}}

\displaystyle:=\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{v_{0}}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{v_{1}}\cdots\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{v_{k}}

(assuming that the factors on the right hand sides are well-defined).

If
u
and
v
are elements of
\widehat{P}
, and if
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, then
we set

\displaystyle A_{\ell}^{u\rightarrow v}

\displaystyle:=\sum\limits_{\mathbf{p}\text{ is a path from
}u\text{ to }v}A_{\ell}^{\mathbf{p}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}

(15)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{u\rightarrow v}

\displaystyle:=\sum\limits_{\mathbf{p}\text{ is a path from
}u\text{ to }v}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{\mathbf{p}}

(16)

(assuming that all addends on the right hand sides are well-defined).

Example 6.1
.

Let
P=\left[2\right]\times\left[2\right]
and
f\in\mathbb{K}^{\widehat{P}}
be as in Example
3.17
. Then,

\displaystyle\left(1,1\right)^{\sim}

\displaystyle=\left(2,2\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(1,2\right)^{\sim}=\left(2,1\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(2,1\right)^{\sim}=\left(1,2\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(2,2\right)^{\sim}=\left(1,1\right),

\displaystyle\left(1,1\right)_{0}

\displaystyle=f\left(1,1\right)=w,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(1,1\right)_{1}=\left(Rf\right)\left(1,1\right)=a\overline{z}b,

\displaystyle\left(1,1\right)_{2}

\displaystyle=\left(R^{2}f\right)\left(1,1\right)=a\overline{b}z\cdot\overline{x+y}\cdot b,

\displaystyle\left(1,2\right)_{2}

\displaystyle=\left(R^{2}f\right)\left(1,2\right)=a\cdot\overline{x+y}\cdot y\left(\overline{x}+\overline{y}\right)b.

There are only two paths from
\left(2,2\right)
to
\left(1,1\right)
: namely, the path
\left(\left(2,2\right)\gtrdot\left(1,2\right)\gtrdot\left(1,1\right)\right)
and the path
\left(\left(2,2\right)\gtrdot\left(2,1\right)\gtrdot\left(1,1\right)\right)
.
Each of these two paths has three vertices. There are no paths from
\left(1,1\right)
to
\left(2,2\right)
, since we don’t have
\left(1,1\right)\geqslant\left(2,2\right)
. The only path from
0
to
0
is the
trivial path
\left(0\right)
.

We have

\displaystyle A_{0}^{\left(1,1\right)}

\displaystyle=\left(1,1\right)_{0}\cdot\overline{\sum\limits\limits_{u\lessdot\left(1,1\right)}u_{0}}=\left(1,1\right)_{0}\cdot\overline{0_{0}}=w\cdot\overline{a},

\displaystyle A_{0}^{\left(2,2\right)}

\displaystyle=\left(2,2\right)_{0}\cdot\overline{\sum\limits\limits_{u\lessdot\left(2,2\right)}u_{0}}=\left(2,2\right)_{0}\cdot\overline{\left(1,2\right)_{0}+\left(2,1\right)_{0}}=z\cdot\overline{y+x},

\displaystyle A_{1}^{\left(1,1\right)}

\displaystyle=\left(1,1\right)_{1}\cdot\overline{\sum\limits\limits_{u\lessdot\left(1,1\right)}u_{1}}=\left(1,1\right)_{1}\cdot\overline{0_{1}}=a\overline{z}b\cdot\overline{a},

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{0}^{\left(1,1\right)}

\displaystyle=\overline{\sum\limits\limits_{u\gtrdot\left(1,1\right)}\overline{u_{0}}}\cdot\overline{\left(1,1\right)_{0}}=\overline{\overline{\left(1,2\right)_{0}}+\overline{\left(2,1\right)_{0}}}\cdot\overline{\left(1,1\right)_{0}}=\overline{\overline{y}+\overline{x}}\cdot\overline{w},

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{1}^{\left(1,1\right)}

\displaystyle=\overline{\sum\limits\limits_{u\gtrdot\left(1,1\right)}\overline{u_{1}}}\cdot\overline{\left(1,1\right)_{1}}=\overline{\overline{\left(1,2\right)_{1}}+\overline{\left(2,1\right)_{1}}}\cdot\overline{\left(1,1\right)_{1}}

\displaystyle=\overline{\overline{w\overline{y}\left(x+y\right)\overline{z}b}+\overline{w\overline{x}\left(x+y\right)\overline{z}b}}\cdot\overline{a\overline{z}b}=w\cdot\overline{a}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{after simplifications}\right).

Furthermore, for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\displaystyle A_{\ell}^{\left(\left(2,2\right)\gtrdot\left(1,2\right)\gtrdot\left(1,1\right)\right)}

\displaystyle=A_{\ell}^{\left(2,2\right)}A_{\ell}^{\left(1,2\right)}A_{\ell}^{\left(1,1\right)};

\displaystyle A_{\ell}^{\left(2,2\right)\rightarrow\left(1,1\right)}

\displaystyle=A_{\ell}^{\left(\left(2,2\right)\gtrdot\left(1,2\right)\gtrdot\left(1,1\right)\right)}+A_{\ell}^{\left(\left(2,2\right)\gtrdot\left(2,1\right)\gtrdot\left(1,1\right)\right)}

\displaystyle=A_{\ell}^{\left(2,2\right)}A_{\ell}^{\left(1,2\right)}A_{\ell}^{\left(1,1\right)}+A_{\ell}^{\left(2,2\right)}A_{\ell}^{\left(2,1\right)}A_{\ell}^{\left(1,1\right)}

(and similarly for

\textstyle A

instead of
A
).

The letter
\ell
will always stand for a nonnegative integer (but will not be fixed).

Remark 6.2
.

The elements
A_{\ell}^{v}
and
\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{v}
(for
v\in P

and
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
) are not entirely new. They are closely connected with
the down-transfer operator
\nabla
and the up-transfer operator
\Delta

studied in
[
JosRob20
, Definition 5.11]
; to be specific, we have

A_{\ell}^{v}=\left(\nabla R^{\ell}f\right)\left(v\right)

and
\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{v}=\left(\Delta\Theta R^{\ell}f\right)\left(v\right)
using the notations of
[
JosRob20
, Definition 5.11]
. These
operators
\nabla
and
\Delta
have a long history, going back to Stanley’s
“transfer map”
\phi
between the order
polytope and the chain polytope of a poset (see
[
Stan86
, Definition
3.1]
). The down-transfer operator
\nabla
does indeed
restrict to
\phi
when
\mathbb{K}
is an appropriate tropical semiring. For
this reason, we have been informally referring to
A_{\ell}^{v}
and

\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{v}
as the
down-slack
and the
up-slack
of
v

at time
\ell
(harkening back to the notion of slack from linear
optimization). Arguably, the behavior of these operators when
\mathbb{K}
is
the tropical semiring is not very indicative of the general case.

When
\mathbb{K}
is commutative, our
A_{0}^{v}
have also
implicitly appeared in
[
MusRob17
]
: If
v=\left(i,j\right)\in P
, then

A_{0}^{v}=\overline{A_{ij}}
, where
A_{ij}
is defined as in

[
MusRob17
, (1)]
.

7
Proof of reciprocity: simple lemmas

Throughout this section, we use the notations introduced in Section

6
.

Let us prove some relations between the elements we have introduced. We begin
with a well-definedness result:

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
\ell\geqslant 1
and

R^{\ell}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
. Assume furthermore that
a
is invertible. Let

v\in\widehat{P}
. Then:

(a)

The element
v_{\ell}
is well-defined and invertible.

(b)

The element
v_{\ell-1}
is well-defined and invertible.

(c)

The element
A_{\ell-1}^{v}
is well-defined and invertible.

(d)

The element
\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{v}
is well-defined and invertible.

Proof 7.2
.

From
R^{\ell}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
, we obtain
R^{\ell-1}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
. Hence,
Corollary
3.22
yields that
\left(R^{\ell-1}f\right)\left(0\right)=f\left(0\right)=a
, which is invertible by assumption.

(a)
Clearly,
v_{\ell}=\left(R^{\ell}f\right)\left(v\right)

is well-defined, and we have
v_{\ell}=\left(R\left(R^{\ell-1}f\right)\right)\left(v\right)
. Hence, Lemma
3.35
(applied to

R^{\ell-1}f
instead of
f
) yields that
v_{\ell}
is invertible.

(b)
If
v=0
, then this follows from part
(a)
, because
(
10
) yields that
v_{\ell-1}=a=v_{\ell}
in this case. An
analogous argument works if
v=1
. Thus, we WLOG assume that
v\notin\left\{0,1\right\}
, so that
v\in P
. The element
v_{\ell-1}=\left(R^{\ell-1}f\right)\left(v\right)
is clearly well-defined, and is invertible by
Lemma
3.25
(applied to
R^{\ell-1}f
instead of
f
).

(c)
If
v\in\left\{0,1\right\}
, then this follows from
(
14
). Otherwise,
v\in P
. Applying (
13
) to
\ell-1

instead of
\ell
, we obtain

v_{\ell}=\left(\sum\limits\limits_{u\lessdot v}u_{\ell-1}\right)\cdot\overline{v_{\ell-1}}\cdot\overline{\sum\limits\limits_{u\gtrdot v}\overline{u_{\ell}}}.

(17)

This equality shows that
\overline{v_{\ell-1}}
and
\overline{\sum\limits\limits_{u\gtrdot v}\overline{u_{\ell}}}
are well-defined, i.e., the elements

v_{\ell-1}
and
\sum\limits\limits_{u\gtrdot v}\overline{u_{\ell}}
are invertible.
Also,
v_{\ell}
is invertible (by Lemma
7.1

(a)
).

Solving the equality (
17
) for the first factor
on its right hand side, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum\limits\limits_{u\lessdot v}u_{\ell-1}=v_{\ell}\cdot\left(\sum\limits\limits_{u\gtrdot v}\overline{u_{\ell}}\right)\cdot v_{\ell-1}.

The right hand side of this equality is a product of three invertible
elements; thus, both sides are invertible. Therefore, the element

\overline{\sum\limits\limits_{u\lessdot v}u_{\ell-1}}
is well-defined, hence
invertible (since an inverse is always invertible).

Finally,
A_{\ell-1}^{v}
is defined to be the product
v_{\ell-1}\cdot\overline{\sum\limits\limits_{u\lessdot v}u_{\ell-1}}
, and thus is
well-defined and invertible because both of its factors are.

(d)
If
v\in\left\{0,1\right\}
, then this follows from
(
14
). Otherwise,
v\in P
.

Lemma
7.1

(a)
shows that
v_{\ell}
is invertible;
hence,
\overline{v_{\ell}}
is well-defined, and invertible. Also, in the
proof of Lemma
7.1

(c)
, we have shown that

\overline{\sum\limits\limits_{u\gtrdot v}\overline{u_{\ell}}}
is well-defined, so
it too is invertible. Finally,
\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{v}
is defined to be the
product
\overline{\sum\limits\limits_{u\gtrdot v}\overline{u_{\ell}}}\cdot\overline{v_{\ell}}
, and thus is also well-defined and invertible because
both of its factors are.

Next we show some simple recursions for
A_{\ell}^{s\rightarrow t}

and
\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{s\rightarrow t}
:

Proposition 7.3
.

Let
s
and
t
be two distinct elements of

\widehat{P}
, and fix
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. Then

\displaystyle A_{\ell}^{s\rightarrow t}

\displaystyle=A_{\ell}^{s}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
s\gtrdot u\end{subarray}}A_{\ell}^{u\rightarrow t}

(18)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
u\gtrdot t\end{subarray}}A_{\ell}^{s\rightarrow u}A_{\ell}^{t}

(19)

and

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{s\rightarrow t}

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{s}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
s\gtrdot u\end{subarray}}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{u\rightarrow t}

(20)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
u\gtrdot t\end{subarray}}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{s\rightarrow u}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{t}.

(21)

Here, we assume that all the terms in the respective equalities are well-defined.

Proof 7.4
.

Since
s\neq t
, every path from
s
to
t
must contain an element covered by

s
as its second vertex.

Fix an element
u\in\widehat{P}
satisfying
s\gtrdot u
. If
\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{k}\right)
is a path from
s
to

t
satisfying
v_{1}=u
, then
\left(v_{1}\gtrdot v_{2}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{k}\right)
is a path from
u
to
t
. Hence, we have found a map

\displaystyle\text{from }\left\{\text{paths }\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{k}\right)\text{ from }s\text{ to }t\text{ satisfying }v_{1}=u\right\}

\displaystyle\text{to }\left\{\text{paths from }u\text{ to }t\right\}

that sends each path
\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{k}\right)
to
\left(v_{1}\gtrdot v_{2}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{k}\right)
. This map is a bijection (since any path from
u
to
t
can
be uniquely extended to a path from
s
to
t
by inserting the vertex
s
at
the front). We can use this bijection to substitute
\left(v_{1}\gtrdot v_{2}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{k}\right)
for
\mathbf{p}
in a sum that
ranges over all paths
\mathbf{p}
from
u
to
t
. In particular,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\mathbf{p}\text{ is a path from }u\text{ to }t}A_{\ell}^{s}A_{\ell}^{\mathbf{p}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{k}\right)\text{ is a path from }s\text{ to }t;\\
v_{1}=u\end{subarray}}\underbrace{A_{\ell}^{s}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=A_{\ell}^{v_{0}}\\
\text{(since }s=v_{0}\text{)}\end{subarray}}\underbrace{A_{\ell}^{\left(v_{1}\gtrdot v_{2}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{k}\right)}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=A_{\ell}^{v_{1}}A_{\ell}^{v_{2}}\cdots A_{\ell}^{v_{k}}\\
\text{(by the definition of }A_{\ell}^{\left(v_{1}\gtrdot v_{2}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{k}\right)}\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{k}\right)\text{ is a path from }s\text{ to }t;\\
v_{1}=u\end{subarray}}\underbrace{A_{\ell}^{v_{0}}A_{\ell}^{v_{1}}A_{\ell}^{v_{2}}\cdots A_{\ell}^{v_{k}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=A_{\ell}^{\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{k}\right)}\\
\text{(by the definition of }A_{\ell}^{\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{k}\right)}\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{k}\right)\text{ is a path from }s\text{ to }t;\\
v_{1}=u\end{subarray}}A_{\ell}^{\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{k}\right)}.

(22)

Now, forget that we fixed
u
. We thus have proved
(
22
) for each
u\in\widehat{P}
satisfying

s\gtrdot u
.

The definition of
A_{\ell}^{s\rightarrow t}
yields

\displaystyle A_{\ell}^{s\rightarrow t}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{k}\right)\text{ is a path from }s\text{ to }t}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
s\gtrdot u\end{subarray}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{k}\right)\text{ is a path from }s\text{ to }t;\\
v_{1}=u\end{subarray}}\\
\text{(because any path
}\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{k}\right)\text{ from
}s\text{ to }t\text{ has a well-defined}\\
\text{second vertex }v_{1}\text{,
and this second vertex }v_{1}\text{ satisfies }s\gtrdot v_{1}\text{)}\end{subarray}}A_{\ell}^{\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{k}\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
s\gtrdot u\end{subarray}}\ \ \underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{k}\right)\text{ is a path from }s\text{ to }t;\\
v_{1}=u\end{subarray}}A_{\ell}^{\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{k}\right)}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\mathbf{p}\text{ is a path from }u\text{ to }t}A_{\ell}^{s}A_{\ell}^{\mathbf{p}}\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.prop.slacks.rec.short.1}))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
s\gtrdot u\end{subarray}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\mathbf{p}\text{
is a path from }u\text{ to }t}A_{\ell}^{s}A_{\ell}^{\mathbf{p}}=A_{\ell}^{s}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
s\gtrdot u\end{subarray}}\ \ \underbrace{\sum\limits_{\mathbf{p}\text{ is a path from }u\text{ to }t}A_{\ell}^{\mathbf{p}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=A_{\ell}^{u\rightarrow t}\\
\text{(by the definition of }A_{\ell}^{u\rightarrow t}\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=A_{\ell}^{s}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
s\gtrdot u\end{subarray}}A_{\ell}^{u\rightarrow t}.

This proves (
18
). The same argument (but with each

A
symbol replaced by an

\textstyle A

symbol) proves
(
20
). Moreover, a similar argument (but now
classifying paths from
s
to
t
according to their second-to-last vertex
instead of their second vertex) establishes (
19
)
and (
21
). Thus, Proposition
7.3
is proven.

The next proposition uses the products
\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{v}
and

A_{\ell-1}^{v}
to rewrite the equality (
13
) (which is
essentially the definition of birational rowmotion) in a slick way:

Proposition 7.5

(Transition equation in
A
-

\textstyle A

-form)
.

Let
v\in\widehat{P}
and
\ell\geqslant 1
be such that
R^{\ell}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
.
Assume that
a
is invertible. Then,

\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{v}=A_{\ell-1}^{v}.

Proof 7.6
.

If
v
is
0
or
1
, then the equality
\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{v}=A_{\ell-1}^{v}
holds because both of its sides are
1
(by (
14
)).
Thus, we assume WLOG that
v\in P
.

Lemma
7.1

(a)
yields that
v_{\ell}
is well-defined
and invertible, while Lemma
7.1

(c,d)
yield that

\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{v}
and
A_{\ell-1}^{v}
are well-defined. Since

A_{\ell-1}^{v}
is defined as
v_{\ell-1}\cdot\overline{\sum\limits\limits_{u\lessdot v}u_{\ell-1}}
, this entails that
\sum\limits\limits_{u\lessdot v}u_{\ell-1}
is invertible.

If
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta
are four invertible elements of
\mathbb{K}

satisfying
\alpha=\beta\overline{\gamma}\delta
, then

\delta\overline{\alpha}=\delta\underbrace{\overline{\beta\overline{\gamma}\delta}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\overline{\delta}\gamma\overline{\beta}\\
\text{}\end{subarray}}=\underbrace{\delta\overline{\delta}}_{=1}\gamma\overline{\beta}=\gamma\overline{\beta}.

(23)

Applying (
13
) to
\ell-1
instead of
\ell
, we find

v_{\ell}=\left(\sum\limits\limits_{u\lessdot v}u_{\ell-1}\right)\cdot\overline{v_{\ell-1}}\cdot\overline{\sum\limits\limits_{u\gtrdot v}\overline{u_{\ell}}}.

Thus, (
23
) (applied to
\alpha=v_{\ell}
,

\beta=\sum\limits\limits_{u\lessdot v}u_{\ell-1}
,
\gamma=v_{\ell-1}
and

\delta=\overline{\sum\limits\limits_{u\gtrdot v}\overline{u_{\ell}}}
) yields

\overline{\sum\limits\limits_{u\gtrdot v}\overline{u_{\ell}}}\cdot\overline{v_{\ell}}=v_{\ell-1}\cdot\overline{\sum\limits\limits_{u\lessdot v}u_{\ell-1}}.

But the left hand side of this equality is
\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{v}
(by the
definition of
\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{v}
), whereas the right hand side is

A_{\ell-1}^{v}
. Hence, this equality simplifies to
\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{v}=A_{\ell-1}^{v}
. This proves Proposition
7.5
.

As a consequence of Proposition
7.5
, we have:

Corollary 7.7
.

Let
\mathbf{p}
be a path. Let
\ell\geqslant 1
be such
that
R^{\ell}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
. Assume that
a
is invertible. Then,

\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{\mathbf{p}}=A_{\ell-1}^{\mathbf{p}}.

Corollary 7.8
.

Let
u,v\in\widehat{P}
. Let
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
be
such that
\ell\geqslant 1
and
R^{\ell}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
. Assume that
a
is invertible.
Then,

\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{u\rightarrow v}=A_{\ell-1}^{u\rightarrow v}.

(24)

The next theorem gives ways to recover the labels
u_{\ell}=\left(R^{\ell}f\right)\left(u\right)
from some of the sums defined in
(
15
) and (
16
).
19
19

19

The condition

\ell\geqslant 1
in Theorem
7.9

(a)
and
(c)
is
meant to ensure that
\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{1\rightarrow u}
and
\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{\left(p,q\right)\rightarrow u}
are invertible. It can be replaced by
directly requiring the latter.

Theorem 7.9

(path formulas for rectangle)
.

Let
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.
Assume that
a
is invertible. Then:

(a)

If
R^{\ell}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
and
\ell\geqslant 1
, then each
u\in P
satisfies

u_{\ell}=\overline{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{1\rightarrow u}}\cdot b

(and the inverse
\overline{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{1\rightarrow u}}
is well-defined).

(b)

If
R^{\ell+1}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
, then each
u\in P
satisfies

u_{\ell}=A_{\ell}^{u\rightarrow 0}\cdot a.

(c)

If
R^{\ell}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
and
\ell\geqslant 1
, then each
u\in P
satisfies

u_{\ell}=\overline{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{\left(p,q\right)\rightarrow u}}\cdot b

(and the inverse
\overline{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{\left(p,q\right)\rightarrow u}}
is well-defined).

(d)

If
R^{\ell+1}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
, then each
u\in P
satisfies

u_{\ell}=A_{\ell}^{u\rightarrow\left(1,1\right)}\cdot a.

Proof 7.10

(Proof of Theorem
7.9
.)
.

(a)
Assume that
R^{\ell}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
and
\ell\geqslant 1
. Then, Lemma
7.1

(d)

yields that the element
\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{v}
is well-defined and invertible
for each
v\in\widehat{P}
. Hence, the element
\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{\mathbf{p}}

is well-defined for each path
\mathbf{p}
. Therefore, the element

\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{1\rightarrow u}
is well-defined for each
u\in P
.

Next, we will prove the equality

\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{1\rightarrow u}=b\overline{u_{\ell}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }u\in P.

(25)

(The
\overline{u_{\ell}}
on the right hand side here is well-defined, since
Lemma
7.1

(a)
(applied to
v=u
) shows that
u_{\ell}
is well-defined and invertible.)

Proof 7.11

(Proof of (
25
).)
.

We utilize downwards induction on
u
.
This is a version of strong induction in which we fix an element
v\in P
and
assume (as the induction hypothesis) that (
25
) holds
for all
u\in P
satisfying
u>v
. We will then prove that
(
25
) also holds for
u=v
. Since the poset
P
is
finite, this will entail that (
25
) holds for all
u\in P
.

Let
v\in P
. Assume (as the induction hypothesis) that
(
25
) holds for all
u\in P
satisfying
u>v
. In other
words, we have
\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{1\rightarrow u}=b\overline{u_{\ell}}
for
each
u\in P
satisfying
u>v
. Thus, in particular, we have

\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{1\rightarrow u}=b\overline{u_{\ell}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }u\in P\text{ satisfying }u\gtrdot v.

(26)

Note also that the only path from
1
to
1
is the trivial path
\left(1\right)
. Hence,

\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{1\rightarrow 1}=\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{\left(1\right)}=\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{1}=1=b\overline{1_{\ell}}

(27)

(since
1_{\ell}=b
).

However,
1\neq v
(since
1\notin P
and
v\in P
). Thus,
(
21
) (applied to
s=1
and
t=v
) yields

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{1\rightarrow v}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
u\gtrdot v\end{subarray}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{u\gtrdot v}\\
\text{(since our
sums}\\
\text{range over }\widehat{P}\text{ by}\\
\text{default)}\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{1\rightarrow u}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=b\overline{u_{\ell}}\\
\text{(indeed, this follows from (\ref{pf.thm.rect.path.a.1.pf.IH2}) when
}u\in P\text{,}\\
\text{and follows from (\ref{pf.thm.rect.path.a.1.pf.IH3})
when }u=1\text{;}\\
\text{and there are no other possibilities, since }u\gtrdot v\text{ rules out }u=0\text{)}\end{subarray}}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{v}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{u\gtrdot v}b\overline{u_{\ell}}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{v}=b\left(\sum\limits_{u\gtrdot v}\overline{u_{\ell}}\right)\underbrace{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{v}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\overline{\sum\limits\limits_{u\gtrdot v}\overline{u_{\ell}}}\cdot\overline{v_{\ell}}\\
\text{(by the definition of }\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{v}\text{)}\end{subarray}}=b\underbrace{\left(\sum\limits_{u\gtrdot v}\overline{u_{\ell}}\right)\overline{\sum\limits\limits_{u\gtrdot v}\overline{u_{\ell}}}}_{=1}\cdot\overline{v_{\ell}}=b\overline{v_{\ell}}.

In other words, (
25
) holds for
u=v
. This completes
the induction step. Thus, we have proved (
25
) by induction.

Note that
1_{\ell}
is invertible (by Lemma
7.1

(a)
,
applied to
v=1
). In other words,
b
is invertible (since
1_{\ell}=b
).

Now, let
u\in P
. Then,
b\overline{u_{\ell}}
is invertible (since
b
and

\overline{u_{\ell}}
are). In view of (
25
), this
means that
\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{1\rightarrow u}
is invertible. Hence,

\overline{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{1\rightarrow u}}
is well-defined. Solving
(
25
) for
u_{\ell}
, we thus obtain
u_{\ell}=\overline{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{1\rightarrow u}}\cdot b
. This proves Theorem

7.9

(a)
.

(b)
This proof is rather similar to that of part
(a)
, but
uses upwards induction instead of downwards induction (and applies
(
18
) instead of (
21
)).

(c)
Let
u\in P
. Recall that
\left(p,q\right)
is the
unique maximal element of
P
. Therefore, each path from
1
to
u
begins
with the step
1\gtrdot\left(p,q\right)
. Thus,
\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{1\rightarrow u}=\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{\left(p,q\right)\rightarrow u}
(since

\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{1}=1
). Hence, part
(c)
follows from
(a)
.

Similarly, part
(d)
follows from
(b)
.

Remark 7.12
.

Corollary
7.8
, Proposition

7.3
and parts
(a)
and
(b)
of Theorem

7.9
hold more generally if
P
is replaced by any finite poset
(not necessarily a rectangle). The proofs we gave above work in that
generality. Parts
(c)
and
(d)
of Theorem
7.9

can be similarly generalized as long as the poset
P
has a global maximum
(for part
(c)
) and a global minimum (for part
(d)
); all we
need to do is to replace
\left(p,q\right)
by the global maximum and

\left(1,1\right)
by the global minimum. We will have no need for this
generality, though.

8
Proof of reciprocity: the case
\left(i,j\right)=\left(1,1\right)

Now, we are mostly ready to prove that Theorem
4.6
holds in
the case when
\left(i,j\right)=\left(1,1\right)
. For reasons both
technical and pedagogical, it is useful for us to dispose of this case now in
order to have less work to do later. First, we prove Theorem

4.6
for
\left(i,j\right)=\left(1,1\right)
under the
extra assumption that
a
is invertible:

Lemma 8.1
.

Assume that
P
is the
p\times q
-rectangle

\left[p\right]\times\left[q\right]
. Let
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
be such
that
\ell\geqslant 1
. Let
f\in\mathbb{K}^{\widehat{P}}
be a
\mathbb{K}
-labeling such that
R^{\ell}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
. Let
a=f\left(0\right)
and

b=f\left(1\right)
. Assume that
a
is invertible. Then,

\left(R^{\ell}f\right)\left(1,1\right)=a\cdot\overline{\left(R^{\ell-1}f\right)\left(p,q\right)}\cdot b.

Proof 8.2
.

We use the notations from Section
6
. Thus,
\left(R^{\ell}f\right)\left(1,1\right)=\left(1,1\right)_{\ell}
and

\left(R^{\ell-1}f\right)\left(p,q\right)=\left(p,q\right)_{\ell-1}=A_{\ell-1}^{\left(p,q\right)\rightarrow\left(1,1\right)}\cdot a

(by Theorem
7.9

(d)
, applied to
\ell-1
and
\left(p,q\right)
instead of
\ell
and
u
). Solving this equation for

A_{\ell-1}^{\left(p,q\right)\rightarrow\left(1,1\right)}
,
we obtain

A_{\ell-1}^{\left(p,q\right)\rightarrow\left(1,1\right)}=\left(R^{\ell-1}f\right)\left(p,q\right)\cdot\overline{a}

(28)

(since
a
is invertible). Note also that
R\left(R^{\ell-1}f\right)=R^{\ell}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
, and thus
\left(R^{\ell-1}f\right)\left(p,q\right)
is invertible (by Lemma
3.25
, applied to
R^{\ell-1}f
and
\left(p,q\right)
instead of
f
and
v
).

Now,

\displaystyle\left(R^{\ell}f\right)\left(1,1\right)

\displaystyle=\left(1,1\right)_{\ell}=\overline{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{\left(p,q\right)\rightarrow\left(1,1\right)}}\cdot b\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by Theorem
\ref{thm.rect.path} {\boldmath(c)}, applied to }u=\left(1,1\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\overline{A_{\ell-1}^{\left(p,q\right)\rightarrow\left(1,1\right)}}\cdot b\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since
(\ref{eq.prop.rect.trans.uv}) yields }\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{\left(p,q\right)\rightarrow\left(1,1\right)}=A_{\ell-1}^{\left(p,q\right)\rightarrow\left(1,1\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\overline{\left(R^{\ell-1}f\right)\left(p,q\right)\cdot\overline{a}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=a\cdot\overline{\left(R^{\ell-1}f\right)\left(p,q\right)}\\
\text{(since }\left(R^{\ell-1}f\right)\left(p,q\right)\text{ and }\overline{a}\\
\text{are invertible)}\end{subarray}}\cdot\,b\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by (\ref{pf.lem.rect.antip.11inv.1})}\right)

\displaystyle=a\cdot\overline{\left(R^{\ell-1}f\right)\left(p,q\right)}\cdot b.

This proves Lemma
8.1
.

Unfortunately, our proof of Lemma
8.1
made use of the
requirement that
a
be invertible, since
\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{\left(p,q\right)\rightarrow\left(1,1\right)}
and
A_{\ell-1}^{\left(p,q\right)\rightarrow\left(1,1\right)}
would not be
well-defined otherwise. In order to remove this requirement, we make use of a
trick, in which we “temporarily” set the
label
f\left(0\right)
to
1
and then argue that this has a predictable
effect on
\left(Rf\right)\left(1,1\right)
. This trick relies on the following:

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
P
be an arbitrary finite poset (not necessarily
\left[p\right]\times\left[q\right]
). Let
f,g\in\mathbb{K}^{\widehat{P}}
be
two
\mathbb{K}
-labelings such that
Rf\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
. Assume that

g\left(x\right)=f\left(x\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each
}x\in\widehat{P}\setminus\left\{0\right\}.

(29)

Assume furthermore that
g\left(0\right)=1
. Set
a=f\left(0\right)
. Then:

(a)

We have
Rg\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
.

(b)

If
v\in P
is not a minimal element of
P
, then
\left(Rf\right)\left(v\right)=\left(Rg\right)\left(v\right)
.

(c)

If
v\in P
is a minimal element of
P
, then
\left(Rf\right)\left(v\right)=a\cdot\left(Rg\right)\left(v\right)
.

Proof 8.4

(Proof of Lemma
8.3
(sketched).)
.

Our assumption
(
29
) shows that the labels of
f
equal the corresponding
labels of
g
at all elements of
\widehat{P}
other than at
0
. Only the
labels at
0
can differ.

Compute the labelings
Rf
and
Rg
recursively, as we did in Example

3.17
, making sure to pick a linear extension of
P
that
starts with all minimal elements of
P
(so that the toggles at these minimal
elements all happen at the very end of our computation). The computation for

Rf
proceeds identically with the computation for
Rg
until we
“interact with” the different labels at
0

– that is, until the labels
f\left(0\right)
and
g\left(0\right)

make an appearance in the sums
\sum\limits\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
u\lessdot v\end{subarray}}f\left(u\right)
and
\sum\limits\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
u\lessdot v\end{subarray}}g\left(u\right)
, respectively (because all
other labels of
f
equal the corresponding labels of
g
). However, this
“interaction” only happens when we toggle
at a minimal element of
P
(since
v
has to be minimal in order for

f\left(0\right)
to be an addend of the sum
\sum\limits\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
u\lessdot v\end{subarray}}f\left(u\right)
). Furthermore, when we do
toggle at a minimal element
v
of
P
, the relevant sums
\sum\limits\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
u\lessdot v\end{subarray}}f\left(u\right)
and

\sum\limits\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
u\lessdot v\end{subarray}}g\left(u\right)

simplify to
f\left(0\right)=a
and
g\left(0\right)=1
, respectively
(because
0
is the
only
element
u\in\widehat{P}
satisfying

u\lessdot v
). Therefore, the labels of
Rf
and
Rg
at
v
end up differing
by a factor of
a
(more precisely, the value of
Rf
at
v
ends up being
a

times the label of
Rg
at
v
). This proves Lemma
8.3
.

Let us now get rid of the “
a
is invertible” requirement in Lemma
8.1
:

Lemma 8.5
.

Assume that
P
is the
p\times q
-rectangle

\left[p\right]\times\left[q\right]
. Let
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
be such
that
\ell\geqslant 1
. Let
f\in\mathbb{K}^{\widehat{P}}
be a
\mathbb{K}
-labeling such that
R^{\ell}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
. Let
a=f\left(0\right)
and

b=f\left(1\right)
. Then,

\left(R^{\ell}f\right)\left(1,1\right)=a\cdot\overline{\left(R^{\ell-1}f\right)\left(p,q\right)}\cdot b.

Proof 8.6
.

If
R^{2}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
, then Lemma
3.29
yields that
a
and
b
are
invertible (since
a=f\left(0\right)
and
b=f\left(1\right)
), and
therefore our claim follows directly from Lemma
8.1
.
For this reason, we WLOG assume that
R^{2}f=\operatorname*{\bot}
. If we had
\ell\geqslant 2
,
then we would thus conclude that
R^{\ell}f=\operatorname*{\bot}
as well, which would
contradict
R^{\ell}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
. Hence, we must have
\ell<2
, so that

\ell=1
. Therefore,
R^{\ell-1}=R^{1-1}=R^{0}=\operatorname*{id}
and
consequently
\left(R^{\ell-1}f\right)\left(p,q\right)=f\left(p,q\right)
. Also,
R^{\ell}=R
(since
\ell=1
). Hence,
R=R^{\ell}
, so
that
Rf=R^{\ell}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
.

Now, let
g\in\mathbb{K}^{\widehat{P}}
be the
\mathbb{K}
-labeling that is
obtained from
f
by replacing the label
f\left(0\right)
by
1
. Thus,
we have

g\left(x\right)=f\left(x\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each
}x\in\widehat{P}\setminus\left\{0\right\},

(30)

and we have
g\left(0\right)=1
. Then, Lemma
8.3

(a)

yields
Rg\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
. In other words,
R^{1}g\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
.

We have
\left(p,q\right)\in P\subseteq\widehat{P}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
. Hence, (
30
) yields
g\left(p,q\right)=f\left(p,q\right)
.

We also have
1\in\widehat{P}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
. Thus,
(
30
) yields
g\left(1\right)=f\left(1\right)=b
, so that
b=g\left(1\right)
. Also,
1=g\left(0\right)
,
and clearly
1
is invertible. Hence, Lemma
8.1

(applied to
1
,
g
and
1
instead of
\ell
,
f
and
a
) yields

\left(R^{1}g\right)\left(1,1\right)=1\cdot\overline{\left(R^{1-1}g\right)\left(p,q\right)}\cdot b=\overline{\left(R^{1-1}g\right)\left(p,q\right)}\cdot b.

In view of
R^{1}=R
and
R^{1-1}=\operatorname*{id}
, we can rewrite this as

\left(Rg\right)\left(1,1\right)=\overline{g\left(p,q\right)}\cdot b.

However,
\left(1,1\right)
is a minimal element of
P
. Thus, Lemma

8.3

(c)
(applied to
v=\left(1,1\right)
) yields

\left(Rf\right)\left(1,1\right)=a\cdot\underbrace{\left(Rg\right)\left(1,1\right)}_{=\overline{g\left(p,q\right)}\cdot b}=a\cdot\overline{g\left(p,q\right)}\cdot b=a\cdot\overline{f\left(p,q\right)}\cdot b\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }g\left(p,q\right)=f\left(p,q\right)\right).

In view of
R^{\ell}=R
and
\left(R^{\ell-1}f\right)\left(p,q\right)=f\left(p,q\right)
, we can rewrite this as

\left(R^{\ell}f\right)\left(1,1\right)=a\cdot\overline{\left(R^{\ell-1}f\right)\left(p,q\right)}\cdot b.

Thus, Lemma
8.5
is proven.

This settles the easiest case of Theorem
4.6
– namely, the
case
\left(i,j\right)=\left(1,1\right)
. To get a grip on the general
case, we need more lemmas.

9
The conversion lemma

We continue using the notations from Section
6
.

Lemma 9.1

(Four neighbors lemma)
.

Let
u,v,w,d
be four adjacent
elements of
P
that are arranged as follows on the Hasse diagram of
P
:

\textstyle{u\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{v\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{w\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{d}

(i.e., we have
d=\left(i,j\right)
,
v=\left(i+1,j\right)
,

w=\left(i,j+1\right)
and
u=\left(i+1,j+1\right)
for some

i\in\left[p-1\right]
and some
j\in\left[q-1\right]
).

Assume that
a
is invertible. Let
\ell\geqslant 1
be such that
R^{\ell+1}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
. Then:

(a)

We have

\overline{v_{\ell}}\cdot\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{d}\cdot d_{\ell}=\overline{u_{\ell}}\cdot A_{\ell}^{u}\cdot w_{\ell}.

(b)

We have

\overline{w_{\ell}}\cdot\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{d}\cdot d_{\ell}=\overline{u_{\ell}}\cdot A_{\ell}^{u}\cdot v_{\ell}.

Proof 9.2
.

(a)
We have
R\left(R^{\ell}f\right)=R^{\ell+1}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}=R\left(\operatorname*{\bot}\right)
and thus
R^{\ell}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
. Hence, Lemma

7.1

(a)
yields that
v_{\ell}
is invertible.
Similarly,
w_{\ell}
and
u_{\ell}
and
d_{\ell}
are invertible. Also,
Lemma
7.1

(d)
(applied to
d
instead of
v
) yields
that the element
\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{d}
is well-defined and invertible.
Moreover, Lemma
7.1

(c)
(applied to
u
and
\ell+1

instead of
v
and
\ell
) yields that the element
A_{\ell}^{u}
is
well-defined and invertible.

The elements
s\in\widehat{P}
that satisfy
s\gtrdot d
are
v
and
w
.
Hence,
\sum\limits\limits_{s\gtrdot d}\overline{s_{\ell}}=\overline{v_{\ell}}+\overline{w_{\ell}}
(where, of course, the sum ranges over
s\in\widehat{P}
). Now, the definition of
\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{d}
yields

\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{d}=\overline{\sum\limits\limits_{s\gtrdot d}\overline{s_{\ell}}}\cdot\overline{d_{\ell}}=\overline{\overline{v_{\ell}}+\overline{w_{\ell}}}\cdot\overline{d_{\ell}}

(31)

(since
\sum\limits\limits_{s\gtrdot d}\overline{s_{\ell}}=\overline{v_{\ell}}+\overline{w_{\ell}}
).

The elements
s\in\widehat{P}
that satisfy
s\lessdot u
are
v
and
w
.
Hence,
\sum\limits\limits_{s\lessdot u}s_{\ell}=v_{\ell}+w_{\ell}
. Now, the
definition of
A_{\ell}^{u}
yields

A_{\ell}^{u}=u_{\ell}\cdot\overline{\sum\limits\limits_{s\lessdot u}s_{\ell}}=u_{\ell}\cdot\overline{v_{\ell}+w_{\ell}}

(32)

(since
\sum\limits\limits_{s\lessdot u}s_{\ell}=v_{\ell}+w_{\ell}
). Since this is
well-defined, the element
v_{\ell}+w_{\ell}
of
\mathbb{K}
must be
invertible. Also, we already know that
v_{\ell}
and
w_{\ell}
are
invertible. Hence, Proposition
2.3

(b)
(applied
to
v_{\ell}
and
w_{\ell}
instead of
a
and
b
) yields that

\overline{v_{\ell}}+\overline{w_{\ell}}
is invertible as well and its
inverse is

\overline{\overline{v_{\ell}}+\overline{w_{\ell}}}=v_{\ell}\cdot\overline{v_{\ell}+w_{\ell}}\cdot w_{\ell}.

Now,

\overline{v_{\ell}}\cdot\underbrace{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{d}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\overline{\overline{v_{\ell}}+\overline{w_{\ell}}}\cdot\overline{d_{\ell}}\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.lem.rect.four.1}))}\end{subarray}}\cdot\,d_{\ell}=\overline{v_{\ell}}\cdot\underbrace{\overline{\overline{v_{\ell}}+\overline{w_{\ell}}}}_{=v_{\ell}\cdot\overline{v_{\ell}+w_{\ell}}\cdot w_{\ell}}\cdot\,\underbrace{\overline{d_{\ell}}\cdot d_{\ell}}_{=1}=\underbrace{\overline{v_{\ell}}\cdot v_{\ell}}_{=1}\cdot\,\overline{v_{\ell}+w_{\ell}}\cdot w_{\ell}=\overline{v_{\ell}+w_{\ell}}\cdot w_{\ell}.

Comparing this with

\overline{u_{\ell}}\cdot\underbrace{A_{\ell}^{u}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=u_{\ell}\cdot\overline{v_{\ell}+w_{\ell}}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.lem.rect.four.2}))}\end{subarray}}\cdot\,w_{\ell}=\underbrace{\overline{u_{\ell}}\cdot u_{\ell}}_{=1}\cdot\,\overline{v_{\ell}+w_{\ell}}\cdot w_{\ell}=\overline{v_{\ell}+w_{\ell}}\cdot w_{\ell},

we obtain
\overline{v_{\ell}}\cdot\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{d}\cdot d_{\ell}=\overline{u_{\ell}}\cdot A_{\ell}^{u}\cdot w_{\ell}
. Thus, Lemma

9.1

(a)
is proved.

(b)
This can be proved by the same argument that we used to prove
part
(a)
(with the roles of
v
and
w
interchanged).

We recall our conventions for drawing the
p\times q
-rectangle
P=\left[p\right]\times\left[q\right]
. In light of these conventions, we shall
refer to the set
\left\{\left(k,q\right)\ \mid\ k\in\left[p\right]\right\}
as the
northeastern edge
of
P
, and to the set
\left\{\left(i,1\right)\ \mid\ i\in\left[p\right]\right\}
as the

southwestern edge
of
P
.

The next lemma is crucial, as it allows us to “convert” between
A
’s and

\textstyle A

’s without
changing the subscript.

Lemma 9.3

(Conversion lemma)
.

Let
u
and
u^{\prime}
be two
elements of the northeastern edge of
P
satisfying
u\gtrdot u^{\prime}

(that is, let
u=\left(k,q\right)
and
u^{\prime}=\left(k-1,q\right)

for some
k\in\left\{2,3,\ldots,p\right\}
). Let
d
and
d^{\prime}
be
two elements of the southwestern edge of
P
satisfying
d\gtrdot d^{\prime}

(that is, let
d=\left(i,1\right)
and
d^{\prime}=\left(i-1,1\right)

for some
i\in\left\{2,3,\ldots,p\right\}
).

Assume that
a
is invertible. Let
\ell\geqslant 1
be such that
R^{\ell+1}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
. Then we have:

A_{\ell}^{u\rightarrow d}=\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{u^{\prime}\rightarrow d^{\prime}}.

Here is an illustration for this lemma:

u
u^{\prime}
d
d^{\prime}

(the red path indexes one addend in the sum
A_{\ell}^{u\rightarrow d}=\sum\limits_{\mathbf{p}\text{ is a path from }u\text{ to }d}A_{\ell}^{\mathbf{p}}
, while the blue path contributes to the sum
\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{u^{\prime}\rightarrow d^{\prime}}=\sum\limits_{\mathbf{p}\text{ is a path from
}u^{\prime}\text{ to }d^{\prime}}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{\mathbf{p}}
).

In the case when
\mathbb{K}
is commutative, Lemma
9.3
was
independently discovered by Johnson and Liu
[
JohLiu22
]
. More precisely,

[
JohLiu22
, Lemma 4.1]
extends it from sums over paths (such as

A_{\ell}^{u\rightarrow d}
and
\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{u^{\prime}\rightarrow d^{\prime}}
) to sums over
k
-tuples of non-intersecting paths.
It is unclear whether this extension can still be made when
\mathbb{K}
is
not commutative (what order should the
A_{\ell}^{v}
’s along
different paths be multiplied in?), but the use of determinants likely
precludes any noncommutative generalization of the proof in
[
JohLiu22
]
.

Proof 9.4

(Proof of Lemma
9.3
.)
.

Let
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. We
“interpolate” between the paths from
u
to

d
and the paths from
u^{\prime}
to
d^{\prime}
using what we call
“path-jump-paths”. To define these formally,
we introduce some more basic notations.

The first coordinate of any
x\in P
will be denoted by
\first x
. Thus,

\first\left(i,j\right)=i
for any
\left(i,j\right)\in P
.

Furthermore, for any
x=\left(i,j\right)\in P
, we define the
rank

of
x
to be the positive integer
i+j-1
. This rank will be denoted by

\rank x
.

We define a new binary relation
\blacktriangleright
on the set
P
as
follows: If
x
and
y
are two elements of
P
, then the relation

x\blacktriangleright y
means “
\rank x=\rank y+1
and

\first x>\first y
”. In other words, the relation

x\blacktriangleright y
means that

\text{if }x=\left(i,j\right)\text{, then }y=\left(i-k,j+k-1\right)\text{ for some }k>0.

Visually speaking, it means that
y
is one step southeast and a (nonnegative)
amount of steps east of
x
(on the Hasse diagram).

We define a
path-jump-path
to be a tuple
\mathbf{p}=\left(v_{0},v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\right)
of elements of
P
along with a chosen
number
i\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,k-1\right\}
such that the chain of
relations

v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{i}\blacktriangleright v_{i+1}\gtrdot v_{i+2}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{k}

holds. We denote this path-jump-path simply by

\mathbf{p}=\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{i}\blacktriangleright v_{i+1}\gtrdot v_{i+2}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{k}\right),

(33)

and we say that this path-jump-path
\mathbf{p}
has
jump at
i
. The
elements
v_{0},v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}
are called the
vertices
of this
path-jump-path. The pairs
\left(v_{j},v_{j+1}\right)
of consecutive
vertices are called the
steps
of this path-jump-path. Such a step

\left(v_{j},v_{j+1}\right)
is said to be a
\gtrdot
-step
if

j\neq i
, and it is said to be a
\blacktriangleright
-step
if
j=i
.

Here is an example of a path-jump-path, where the red edge is the

\blacktriangleright
-step:

(Note that two vertices
x
and
y
can satisfy
x\blacktriangleright y
and

x\gtrdot y
simultaneously. Thus, it can happen that several path-jump-paths
with jumps at different
i
’s contain the same vertices. We nevertheless do
not consider these path-jump-paths to be identical, because we understand a
path-jump-path like (
33
) to “remember” not only its vertices
v_{0},v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}

but also the value of
i
.)

A
path-jump-path from
u
to
d^{\prime}
will mean a
path-jump-path

\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{i}\blacktriangleright v_{i+1}\gtrdot v_{i+2}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{k}\right)
such that
v_{0}=u
and
v_{k}=d^{\prime}
.

We note that if two elements
x
and
y
of
P
satisfy
x\gtrdot y
or

x\blacktriangleright y
, then

\rank y=\rank x-1.

(34)

As a consequence of this fact, successive entries
v_{j-1}
and
v_{j}
in a
path-jump-path
\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{i}\blacktriangleright v_{i+1}\gtrdot v_{i+2}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{k}\right)

always satisfy
\rank\left(v_{j}\right)=\rank\left(v_{j-1}\right)-1

for each
j\in\left[k\right]
. In other words, the ranks of the vertices
of a path-jump-path decrease by
1
at each step.

Hence, the difference in ranks between the first and final entries of a
path-jump-path
\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{i}\blacktriangleright v_{i+1}\gtrdot v_{i+2}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{k}\right)

is one less than its number of entries:

\rank\left(v_{0}\right)-\rank\left(v_{k}\right)=k.

(35)

Let
r:=\rank u-\rank\left(d^{\prime}\right)
. Thus, any path-jump-path
from
u
to
d^{\prime}
must contain exactly
r+1
vertices (by
(
35
)). In other words, any path-jump-path
from
u
to
d^{\prime}
must have the form
\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{i}\blacktriangleright v_{i+1}\gtrdot v_{i+2}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r}\right)
.

We have
R\left(R^{\ell}f\right)=R^{\ell+1}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}=R\left(\operatorname*{\bot}\right)
and thus
R^{\ell}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
. Hence, Lemma

7.1

(a)
yields that
v_{\ell}
is well-defined and
invertible for each
v\in P
. Also, Lemma
7.1

(d)

yields that
\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{v}
is well-defined and invertible for each

v\in P
. Moreover, Lemma
7.1

(c)
(applied to

\ell+1
instead of
\ell
) yields that
A_{\ell}^{v}
is
well-defined and invertible for each
v\in P
.

In this proof, we will not consider any
\mathbb{K}
-labelings other than

R^{\ell}f
. Thus, the only labels we will be using are the labels
v_{\ell}=\left(R^{\ell}f\right)\left(v\right)
for
v\in\widehat{P}
. Thus,
we agree to use the following shorthand notation: If
v\in\widehat{P}
, then
the elements
v_{\ell}
,
\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{v}
and
A_{\ell}^{v}
of

\mathbb{K}
will be denoted simply by
v
,
\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v}
and
A^{v}
, respectively. In other words,
we shall omit subscripts when these
subscripts are
\ell
.
For instance, the product
A_{\ell}^{u}u_{\ell}\overline{u_{\ell}^{\prime}}
will thus be abbreviated as

A^{u}u\overline{u^{\prime}}
.

For any path-jump-path

\mathbf{p}=\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{i}\blacktriangleright v_{i+1}\gtrdot v_{i+2}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r}\right)

that contains
r+1
vertices, we set

E_{\mathbf{p}}:=A^{v_{0}}A^{v_{1}}\cdots A^{v_{i-1}}v_{i}\overline{v_{i+1}}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{i+2}}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{i+3}}\cdots\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{r}}\in\mathbb{K}.

Now we claim the following (again omitting subscripts that are
\ell
):

Claim 1:
We have

A^{u\rightarrow d}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{p}\text{ is a
path-jump-path}\\
\text{from }u\text{ to }d^{\prime}\\
\text{with jump at }r-1\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{p}}.

Claim 2:
We have

\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{u^{\prime}\rightarrow d^{\prime}}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{p}\text{
is a path-jump-path}\\
\text{from }u\text{ to }d^{\prime}\\
\text{with jump at
}0\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{p}}.

Claim 3:
For each
j\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,r-2\right\}
, we have

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{p}\text{ is a path-jump-path}\\
\text{from }u\text{ to
}d^{\prime}\\
\text{with jump at }j\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{p}}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{p}\text{ is a path-jump-path}\\
\text{from }u\text{ to }d^{\prime}\\
\text{with
jump at }j+1\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{p}}.

Before we prove these three claims, let us explain how Lemma

9.3
will follow from them:

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{u^{\prime}\rightarrow d^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{u^{\prime}\rightarrow d^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{p}\text{ is a path-jump-path}\\
\text{from }u\text{
to }d^{\prime}\\
\text{with jump at }0\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{p}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by Claim 2}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{p}\text{ is a path-jump-path}\\
\text{from }u\text{
to }d^{\prime}\\
\text{with jump at }1\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{p}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by Claim 3, applied to }j=0\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{p}\text{ is a path-jump-path}\\
\text{from }u\text{
to }d^{\prime}\\
\text{with jump at }2\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{p}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by Claim 3, applied to }j=1\right)

\displaystyle=\cdots

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{p}\text{ is a path-jump-path}\\
\text{from }u\text{
to }d^{\prime}\\
\text{with jump at }r-1\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{p}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by Claim 3, applied to }j=r-2\right)

\displaystyle=A^{u\rightarrow d}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by Claim
1}\right)

\displaystyle=A_{\ell}^{u\rightarrow d}.

Hence, Lemma
9.3
will follow once Claims 1, 2 and 3 have been
proved. Let us now prove these three claims:

Proof 9.5

(Proof of Claim 1.)
.

We know that
d
lies on the southwestern edge of
P
.
Hence, the only
s\in\widehat{P}
satisfying
s\lessdot d
is
d^{\prime}

(since
d\gtrdot d^{\prime}
). Therefore,
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\widehat{P};\\
s\lessdot d\end{subarray}}s_{\ell}=d_{\ell}^{\prime}
. However, the definition of

A_{\ell}^{d}
shows that
A_{\ell}^{d}=d_{\ell}\cdot\overline{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\widehat{P};\\
s\lessdot d\end{subarray}}s_{\ell}}=d_{\ell}\overline{d_{\ell}^{\prime}}
(since
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\widehat{P};\\
s\lessdot d\end{subarray}}s_{\ell}=d_{\ell}^{\prime}
). Since we omit
subscripts (when these subscripts are
\ell
), we can rewrite this as

A^{d}=d\overline{d^{\prime}}.

(36)

We know that any path-jump-path from
u
to
d^{\prime}
must have the form

\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{i}\blacktriangleright v_{i+1}\gtrdot v_{i+2}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r}\right)
. If such a path-jump-path has jump at
r-1
, then it must have the form

\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r-1}\blacktriangleright v_{r}\right)
; that is, its last step
\left(v_{r-1},v_{r}\right)
is an

\blacktriangleright
-step. However, since it ends at
d^{\prime}
, we must
have
v_{r}=d^{\prime}
and thus
v_{r-1}\blacktriangleright v_{r}=d^{\prime}
. This entails
v_{r-1}=d
(since the only
g\in P
satisfying

g\blacktriangleright d^{\prime}
is
d

20
20

20

This follows easily
from the geographical positions of
d
and
d^{\prime}
on the southwestern
edge of
P
.
), and therefore
\left(v_{r-1},v_{r}\right)=\left(d,d^{\prime}\right)
(since
v_{r}=d^{\prime}
). In other words, the last
step of this path-jump-path is
\left(d,d^{\prime}\right)
.

We have thus shown that if a path-jump-path from
u
to
d^{\prime}
has jump
at
r-1
, then its last step is
\left(d,d^{\prime}\right)
. Hence, any
path-jump-path from
u
to
d^{\prime}
with jump at
r-1
must have the form

\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r-1}\blacktriangleright d^{\prime}\right),

where
\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r-1}\right)
is a
path from
u
to
d
. Conversely, any tuple of the latter form is a
path-jump-path from
u
to
d^{\prime}
with jump at
r-1
(since

d\blacktriangleright d^{\prime}
). Therefore,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{p}\text{ is a path-jump-path}\\
\text{from }u\text{ to
}d^{\prime}\\
\text{with jump at }r-1\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{p}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r-1}\right)\\
\text{is a path from }u\text{ to }d\end{subarray}}\underbrace{E_{\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r-1}\blacktriangleright d^{\prime}\right)}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=A^{v_{0}}A^{v_{1}}\cdots A^{v_{r-2}}v_{r-1}\overline{d^{\prime}}\\
\text{(by the definition of }E_{\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r-1}\blacktriangleright d^{\prime}\right)}\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r-1}\right)\\
\text{is a path from }u\text{ to }d\end{subarray}}A^{v_{0}}A^{v_{1}}\cdots A^{v_{r-2}}\underbrace{v_{r-1}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=d\end{subarray}}\overline{d^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r-1}\right)\\
\text{is a path from }u\text{ to }d\end{subarray}}A^{v_{0}}A^{v_{1}}\cdots A^{v_{r-2}}\underbrace{d\overline{d^{\prime}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=A^{d}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.lem.rect.conv.c1.pf.1}))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r-1}\right)\\
\text{is a path from }u\text{ to }d\end{subarray}}A^{v_{0}}A^{v_{1}}\cdots A^{v_{r-2}}\underbrace{A^{d}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=A^{v_{r-1}}\\
\text{(because }d=v_{r-1}\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r-1}\right)\\
\text{is a path from }u\text{ to }d\end{subarray}}\underbrace{A^{v_{0}}A^{v_{1}}\cdots A^{v_{r-2}}A^{v_{r-1}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=A^{\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r-1}\right)}\\
\text{(by the definition of
}A^{\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r-1}\right)}\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r-1}\right)\\
\text{is a path from }u\text{ to }d\end{subarray}}A^{\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r-1}\right)}=\sum\limits_{\mathbf{p}\text{ is a path from }u\text{ to }d}A^{\mathbf{p}}

\displaystyle=A^{u\rightarrow d}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by the
definition of }A^{u\rightarrow d}\right).

This proves Claim 1.

Proof 9.6

(Proof of Claim 2.)
.

This is analogous to the proof of Claim 1. This time, we
need to argue that if a path-jump-path from
u
to
d^{\prime}
has jump at

0
, then its first step is
\left(u,u^{\prime}\right)
(since the only

g\in P
satisfying
u\blacktriangleright g
is
u^{\prime}
).

Proving Claim 3 is a bit trickier. As an auxiliary result, we first show the following:

Claim 4:
Let
s
and
t
be two elements of
P
. Then,

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
s\blacktriangleright x\gtrdot t\end{subarray}}s\overline{x}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{t}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
s\gtrdot x\blacktriangleright t\end{subarray}}A^{s}x\overline{t}.

(37)

Proof 9.7

(Proof of Claim 4.)
.

First, we observe that an
x\in P
satisfying
s\blacktriangleright x\gtrdot t
cannot exist unless
\rank t=\rank s-2
(because
(
34
) yields that such an
x
must satisfy

\rank x=\rank s-1
and
\rank t=\rank x-1
, whence
\rank t=\rank x-1=\left(\rank s-1\right)-1=\rank s-2
). Hence, the left hand side of the desired equality
(
37
) is an empty sum unless
\rank t=\rank s-2
.
Similarly, the same can be said about the right hand side. Thus,
(
37
) boils down to
0=0
unless
\rank t=\rank s-2
. We
therefore assume WLOG that
\rank t=\rank s-2
. In other words,

\rank s-\rank t=2
. In terms of the way that we draw our poset
P
, this means that the
point
s
lies two rows above the point
t
.

The definition of
\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{t}
yields
\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{t}=\overline{\sum\limits\limits_{x\gtrdot t}\overline{x_{\ell}}}\cdot\overline{t_{\ell}}
. Omitting the subscripts, we can rewrite this as

\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{t}=\overline{\sum\limits\limits_{x\gtrdot t}\overline{x}}\cdot\overline{t}.

(38)

The definition of
A_{\ell}^{s}
yields
A_{\ell}^{s}=s_{\ell}\cdot\overline{\sum\limits\limits_{x\lessdot s}x_{\ell}}
. Omitting the
subscripts, we can rewrite this as

A^{s}=s\cdot\overline{\sum\limits\limits_{x\lessdot s}x}.

(39)

Write the elements
s,t\in P
in the forms
s=\left(i,j\right)
and

t=\left(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\right)
. Then,
\rank s=i+j-1
and
\rank t=i^{\prime}+j^{\prime}-1
. Hence,
\rank s-\rank t=i+j-i^{\prime}-j^{\prime}
, so that
i+j-i^{\prime}-j^{\prime}=\rank s-\rank t=2
. Thus,
j^{\prime}=i+j-i^{\prime}-2
.

We are in one of the following three cases:

Case 1:
We have
i^{\prime}<i-1
.

Case 2:
We have
i^{\prime}=i-1
.

Case 3:
We have
i^{\prime}>i-1
.

Representative examples for these three cases are illustrated in the following
pictures:

Case 1

Case 2

Case 3

\textstyle{s\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{t\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{s\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{t\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{s\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{t\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

(the bullets signify the positions of potential neighbors of
s
and
t
; some
of these positions may fall outside of
P
, but this does not disturb our
argument). In terms of the way we draw our poset
P
, the three cases can be
reformulated as “the point
s
lies further west than

t
” (Case 1), “the point
s
lies due
north of
t
” (Case 2) and “the point
s

lies further east than
t
” (Case 3). Note that two elements

x,y\in P
satisfy
x\blacktriangleright y
if and only if
y
lies one step
south and some arbitrary distance east of
x
in our pictures.

Let us first consider Case 1. In this case, the point
s
lies further west
than
t
. Thus,
s
lies further west than any neighbor of
t
as
well
21
21

21

This becomes fairly clear if you draw the configuration and
recall that
s
lies two rows above
t
(so that
P
has points further east
than
s
but further west than
t
). A rigorous version of this argument
(without reference to pictures) can be found in the detailed version of the
present paper.
. Hence, each element
x
of
P
that satisfies
x\gtrdot t

must satisfy
s\blacktriangleright x
automatically. Therefore, the summation
sign
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
s\blacktriangleright x\gtrdot t\end{subarray}}
can be
simplified to
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
x\gtrdot t\end{subarray}}
, and even further to

\sum\limits_{x\gtrdot t}
(because any
x\in\widehat{P}
that satisfies
x\gtrdot t

must belong to
P
automatically
22
22

22

Indeed, the rank of any such
x

must lie between the ranks of
s
and
t
, and thus
x
cannot be
0
or
1
.
). Hence,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
s\blacktriangleright x\gtrdot t\end{subarray}}s\overline{x}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{t}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{x\gtrdot t}s\overline{x}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{t}=s\left(\sum\limits_{x\gtrdot t}\overline{x}\right)\underbrace{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{t}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\overline{\sum\limits\limits_{x\gtrdot t}\overline{x}}\cdot\overline{t}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.lem.rect.conv.c4.pf.1}))}\end{subarray}}=s\underbrace{\left(\sum\limits_{x\gtrdot t}\overline{x}\right)\overline{\sum\limits\limits_{x\gtrdot t}\overline{x}}}_{=1}\cdot\,\overline{t}

\displaystyle=s\overline{t}.

(40)

Recall again that the point
s
lies further west than
t
. Thus, any neighbor
of
s
lies further west than
t
as well (since
s
lies two rows above
t
).
Hence, each element
x
of
P
that satisfies
s\gtrdot x
must satisfy

x\blacktriangleright t
automatically. Therefore, the summation sign

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
s\gtrdot x\blacktriangleright t\end{subarray}}
can be simplified
to
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
s\gtrdot x\end{subarray}}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
x\lessdot s\end{subarray}}
, and even further to
\sum\limits_{x\lessdot s}
(because any
x\in\widehat{P}

that satisfies
x\lessdot s
must belong to
P
automatically
23
23

23

Indeed,
the rank of any such
x
must lie between the ranks of
s
and
t
, and thus

x
cannot be
0
or
1
.
). Hence,

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
s\gtrdot x\blacktriangleright t\end{subarray}}A^{s}x\overline{t}=\sum\limits_{x\lessdot s}A^{s}x\overline{t}=\underbrace{A^{s}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=s\cdot\overline{\sum\limits\limits_{x\lessdot s}x}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.lem.rect.conv.c4.pf.2}))}\end{subarray}}\left(\sum\limits_{x\lessdot s}x\right)\overline{t}=s\cdot\underbrace{\overline{\sum\limits\limits_{x\lessdot s}x}\left(\sum\limits_{x\lessdot s}x\right)}_{=1}\overline{t}=s\overline{t}.

Comparing this with (
40
), we obtain

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
s\blacktriangleright x\gtrdot t\end{subarray}}s\overline{x}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{t}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
s\gtrdot x\blacktriangleright t\end{subarray}}A^{s}x\overline{t}
. Thus, Claim 4 is proved in Case 1.

Let us now consider Case 2. In this case, we have
i^{\prime}=i-1
. Hence,

j^{\prime}=i+j-\underbrace{i^{\prime}}_{=i-1}-2=i+j-\left(i-1\right)-2=j-1
. Thus,
t=\left(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\right)=\left(i-1,j-1\right)
(since
i^{\prime}=i-1
and
j^{\prime}=j-1
). Let

v:=\left(i,j-1\right)
and
w:=\left(i-1,j\right)
. In our coordinate
system, the four points

s=\left(i,j\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ t=\left(i-1,j-1\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ v=\left(i,j-1\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ w=\left(i-1,j\right)

are arranged in a
1\times 1
-square, which looks as follows:

\textstyle{s\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{v\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{w\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{t}
.

(41)

Hence,
v
and
w
belong to
P
(since
s
and
t
belong to
P
), and
furthermore, Lemma
9.1

(b)
(applied to
s
and
t

instead of
u
and
d
) yields

\overline{w_{\ell}}\cdot\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{t}\cdot t_{\ell}=\overline{s_{\ell}}\cdot A_{\ell}^{s}\cdot v_{\ell}.

Since we are omitting subscripts, we can rewrite this as follows:

\overline{w}\cdot\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{t}\cdot t=\overline{s}\cdot A^{s}\cdot v.

The picture (
41
) shows that we have

s\blacktriangleright w
but not
s\blacktriangleright v
. Hence, there is
only one element
x\in P
that satisfies
s\blacktriangleright x\gtrdot t
;
namely, this element
x
is
w
. Hence,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
s\blacktriangleright x\gtrdot t\end{subarray}}s\overline{x}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{t}

\displaystyle=s\overline{w}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{t}=s\cdot\overline{w}\cdot\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{t}\cdot\underbrace{1}_{=t\cdot\overline{t}}=s\cdot\underbrace{\overline{w}\cdot\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{t}\cdot t}_{=\overline{s}\cdot A^{s}\cdot v}\cdot\,\overline{t}=\underbrace{s\cdot\overline{s}}_{=1}\cdot A^{s}\cdot v\cdot\overline{t}

\displaystyle=A^{s}\cdot v\cdot\overline{t}.

(42)

On the other hand, the picture (
41
) shows that
we have
v\blacktriangleright t
but not
w\blacktriangleright t
. Hence,
there is only one element
x\in P
that satisfies
s\gtrdot x\blacktriangleright t
; namely, this element
x
is
v
. Hence,

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
s\gtrdot x\blacktriangleright t\end{subarray}}A^{s}x\overline{t}=A^{s}v\overline{t}=A^{s}\cdot v\cdot\overline{t}.

Comparing this with (
42
), we obtain
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
s\blacktriangleright x\gtrdot t\end{subarray}}s\overline{x}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{t}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
s\gtrdot x\blacktriangleright t\end{subarray}}A^{s}x\overline{t}
. Thus, Claim 4 is proved in Case 2.

Let us finally consider Case 3. In this case, we have
i^{\prime}>i-1
. Thus,

i^{\prime}\geqslant i
(since
i^{\prime}
and
i
are integers), so that
i\leqslant i^{\prime}
. Note that
i=\first s
(since
s=\left(i,j\right)
) and

i^{\prime}=\first t
(since
t=\left(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\right)
).

There exists no
x\in P
satisfying
s\blacktriangleright x\gtrdot t
(because
if
x\in P
satisfies
s\blacktriangleright x\gtrdot t
, then
x\gtrdot t=\left(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\right)
entails
\first x\geqslant i^{\prime}\geqslant i=\first s
, but this clearly contradicts
s\blacktriangleright x
). Hence,
the sum
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
s\blacktriangleright x\gtrdot t\end{subarray}}s\overline{x}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{t}
is empty. Thus,
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
s\blacktriangleright x\gtrdot t\end{subarray}}s\overline{x}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{t}=0
.

Furthermore, there exists no
x\in P
satisfying
s\gtrdot x\blacktriangleright t
(because if
x\in P
satisfies
s\gtrdot x\blacktriangleright t
, then
\left(i,j\right)=s\gtrdot x
entails
