\first x\leqslant i\leqslant i^{\prime}=\first t
; but this clearly contradicts

x\blacktriangleright t
). Hence, the sum
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
s\gtrdot x\blacktriangleright t\end{subarray}}A^{s}x\overline{t}
is empty. Thus,

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
s\gtrdot x\blacktriangleright t\end{subarray}}A^{s}x\overline{t}=0
.

Comparing this with
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
s\blacktriangleright x\gtrdot t\end{subarray}}s\overline{x}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{t}=0
, we obtain
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
s\blacktriangleright x\gtrdot t\end{subarray}}s\overline{x}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{t}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
s\gtrdot x\blacktriangleright t\end{subarray}}A^{s}x\overline{t}
. Thus, Claim 4 is proved in Case 3.

We have now proved Claim 4 in all three cases.

We can now step to the proof of Claim 3:

Proof 9.8

(Proof of Claim 3.)
.

Let
j\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,r-2\right\}
.

We know that any path-jump-path from
u
to
d^{\prime}
must have the form

\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{i}\blacktriangleright v_{i+1}\gtrdot v_{i+2}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r}\right)
. If such a path-jump-path has jump at
j
, then it must have the form

\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{j}\blacktriangleright v_{j+1}\gtrdot v_{j+2}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r}\right)
. Thus,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{p}\text{ is a path-jump-path}\\
\text{from }u\text{
to }d^{\prime}\\
\text{with jump at }j\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{p}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{j}\blacktriangleright v_{j+1}\gtrdot v_{j+2}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r}\right)\\
\text{is a path-jump-path}\\
\text{from }u\text{ to }d^{\prime}\\
\text{with jump at }j\end{subarray}}\underbrace{E_{\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{j}\blacktriangleright v_{j+1}\gtrdot v_{j+2}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r}\right)}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=A^{v_{0}}A^{v_{1}}\cdots A^{v_{j-1}}v_{j}\overline{v_{j+1}}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{j+2}}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{j+3}}\cdots\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{r}}\\
\text{(by the
definition of }E_{\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{j}\blacktriangleright v_{j+1}\gtrdot v_{j+2}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r}\right)}\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{j}\blacktriangleright v_{j+1}\gtrdot v_{j+2}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r}\right)\\
\text{is a path-jump-path}\\
\text{from }u\text{ to }d^{\prime}\\
\text{with jump at }j\end{subarray}}A^{v_{0}}A^{v_{1}}\cdots A^{v_{j-1}}v_{j}\overline{v_{j+1}}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{j+2}}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{j+3}}\cdots\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{r}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{j}\right)\\
\text{is a path starting at }u\end{subarray}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(v_{j+2}\gtrdot v_{j+3}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r}\right)\\
\text{is a path
ending at }d^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}v_{j+1}\in P;\\
v_{j}\blacktriangleright v_{j+1}\gtrdot v_{j+2}\end{subarray}}A^{v_{0}}A^{v_{1}}\cdots A^{v_{j-1}}v_{j}\overline{v_{j+1}}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{j+2}}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{j+3}}\cdots\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{r}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{here, we have broken up our}\\
\text{path-jump-path }\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{j}\blacktriangleright v_{j+1}\gtrdot v_{j+2}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r}\right)\\
\text{into two paths }\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{j}\right)\text{ and }\left(v_{j+2}\gtrdot v_{j+3}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r}\right)\\
\text{and an intermediate vertex }v_{j+1}\text{ satisfying }v_{j}\blacktriangleright v_{j+1}\gtrdot v_{j+2}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{j}\right)\\
\text{is a path starting at }u\end{subarray}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(v_{j+2}\gtrdot v_{j+3}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r}\right)\\
\text{is a path
ending at }d^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
v_{j}\blacktriangleright x\gtrdot v_{j+2}\end{subarray}}A^{v_{0}}A^{v_{1}}\cdots A^{v_{j-1}}v_{j}\overline{x}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{j+2}}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{j+3}}\cdots\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{r}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{here we have renamed
}v_{j+1}\text{ as }x\text{ in the inner sum}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{j}\right)\\
\text{is a path starting at }u\end{subarray}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(v_{j+2}\gtrdot v_{j+3}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r}\right)\\
\text{is a path
ending at }d^{\prime}\end{subarray}}A^{v_{0}}A^{v_{1}}\cdots A^{v_{j-1}}\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
v_{j}\blacktriangleright x\gtrdot v_{j+2}\end{subarray}}v_{j}\overline{x}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{j+2}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
v_{j}\gtrdot x\blacktriangleright v_{j+2}\end{subarray}}A^{v_{j}}x\overline{v_{j+2}}\\
\text{(by Claim 4,}\\
\text{applied to }s=v_{j}\\
\text{and }t=v_{j+2}\text{)}\end{subarray}}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{j+3}}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{j+4}}\cdots\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{r}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{j}\right)\\
\text{is a path starting at }u\end{subarray}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(v_{j+2}\gtrdot v_{j+3}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r}\right)\\
\text{is a path
ending at }d^{\prime}\end{subarray}}A^{v_{0}}A^{v_{1}}\cdots A^{v_{j-1}}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
v_{j}\gtrdot x\blacktriangleright v_{j+2}\end{subarray}}A^{v_{j}}x\overline{v_{j+2}}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{j+3}}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{j+4}}\cdots\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{r}}.

We know that any path-jump-path from
u
to
d^{\prime}
must have the form

\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{i}\blacktriangleright v_{i+1}\gtrdot v_{i+2}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r}\right)
. If such a path-jump-path has jump at
j+1
, then it must have the form

\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{j+1}\blacktriangleright v_{j+2}\gtrdot v_{j+3}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r}\right)
. Thus,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{p}\text{ is a path-jump-path}\\
\text{from }u\text{
to }d^{\prime}\\
\text{with jump at }j+1\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{p}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{j+1}\blacktriangleright v_{j+2}\gtrdot v_{j+3}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r}\right)\\
\text{is a path-jump-path}\\
\text{from }u\text{ to }d^{\prime}\\
\text{with jump at }j+1\end{subarray}}\underbrace{E_{\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{j+1}\blacktriangleright v_{j+2}\gtrdot v_{j+3}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r}\right)}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=A^{v_{0}}A^{v_{1}}\cdots A^{v_{j}}v_{j+1}\overline{v_{j+2}}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{j+3}}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{j+4}}\cdots\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{r}}\\
\text{(by the
definition of }E_{\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{j+1}\blacktriangleright v_{j+2}\gtrdot v_{j+3}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r}\right)}\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{j+1}\blacktriangleright v_{j+2}\gtrdot v_{j+3}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r}\right)\\
\text{is a path-jump-path}\\
\text{from }u\text{ to }d^{\prime}\\
\text{with jump at }j+1\end{subarray}}A^{v_{0}}A^{v_{1}}\cdots A^{v_{j}}v_{j+1}\overline{v_{j+2}}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{j+3}}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{j+4}}\cdots\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{r}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{j}\right)\\
\text{is a path starting at }u\end{subarray}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(v_{j+2}\gtrdot v_{j+3}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r}\right)\\
\text{is a path
ending at }d^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}v_{j+1}\in P;\\
v_{j}\gtrdot v_{j+1}\blacktriangleright v_{j+2}\end{subarray}}A^{v_{0}}A^{v_{1}}\cdots A^{v_{j}}v_{j+1}\overline{v_{j+2}}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{j+3}}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{j+4}}\cdots\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{r}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{j}\right)\\
\text{is a path starting at }u\end{subarray}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(v_{j+2}\gtrdot v_{j+3}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r}\right)\\
\text{is a path
ending at }d^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
v_{j}\gtrdot x\blacktriangleright v_{j+2}\end{subarray}}A^{v_{0}}A^{v_{1}}\cdots A^{v_{j}}x\overline{v_{j+2}}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{j+3}}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{j+4}}\cdots\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{r}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{here we have renamed
}v_{j+1}\text{ as }x\text{ in the inner sum}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(v_{0}\gtrdot v_{1}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{j}\right)\\
\text{is a path starting at }u\end{subarray}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(v_{j+2}\gtrdot v_{j+3}\gtrdot\cdots\gtrdot v_{r}\right)\\
\text{is a path
ending at }d^{\prime}\end{subarray}}A^{v_{0}}A^{v_{1}}\cdots A^{v_{j-1}}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
v_{j}\gtrdot x\blacktriangleright v_{j+2}\end{subarray}}A^{v_{j}}x\overline{v_{j+2}}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{j+3}}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{j+4}}\cdots\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{v_{r}}.

Comparing our last two equalities, we obtain

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{p}\text{ is a path-jump-path}\\
\text{from }u\text{ to
}d^{\prime}\\
\text{with jump at }j\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{p}}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{p}\text{ is a path-jump-path}\\
\text{from }u\text{ to }d^{\prime}\\
\text{with
jump at }j+1\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{p}}.

Thus, Claim 3 is proven.

We have now proved all three Claims 1, 2 and 3. As we explained, this
completes the proof of Lemma
9.3
.

Remark 9.9
.

Parts of the above proof of Lemma

9.3
can be rewritten in a more abstract (although probably not
shorter) manner, avoiding the notion of a “path-jump-path” and the nested sums that appeared in our
proof of Claim 3.

To rewrite the proof, we need the notion of
P\times P
-matrices.
A
P\times P
-matrix
is a matrix whose rows and columns are indexed not
by integers but by elements of
P
. (That is, it is a family of elements of

\mathbb{K}
indexed by pairs
\left(i,j\right)\in P\times P
.) If
C
is
any
P\times P
-matrix, and if
i
and
j
are two elements of
P
, then the

\left(i,j\right)
-th entry of
C
is denoted by
C_{i,j}
. Addition and
multiplication are defined for
P\times P
-matrices in the same way as they
are for usual matrices. That is, for any
P\times P
-matrices
C
and
D
and
any
\left(i,j\right)\in P\times P
, we have

\left(C+D\right)_{i,j}=C_{i,j}+D_{i,j}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(CD\right)_{i,j}=\sum\limits_{k\in P}C_{i,k}D_{k,j}.

For any statement
\mathcal{A}
, we let
\left[\mathcal{A}\right]
be the
Iverson bracket (i.e., truth value) of
\mathcal{A}
. That is,
\left[\mathcal{A}\right]=1
if
\mathcal{A}
is true, and
\left[\mathcal{A}\right]=0
if
\mathcal{A}
is false.

Now, let
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. Define three
P\times P
-matrices
\mathbf{A}
,

\textstyle\mathbf{A}

and
\mathbf{U}
by

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{x,y}

\displaystyle:=A^{x}\left[x\gtrdot y\right],

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\mathbf{A}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle\mathbf{A}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{A}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{A}$}}_{x,y}

\displaystyle:=\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{y}\left[x\gtrdot y\right],

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}_{x,y}

\displaystyle:=x\overline{y}\left[x\blacktriangleright y\right]\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for all }x,y\in P.

Here, the relation
x\blacktriangleright y
is defined as in the above proof
of Lemma
9.3
, and we are again omitting the “
\ell
” subscripts, so (for instance) “
x\overline{y}
” actually means
x_{\ell}\overline{y_{\ell}}
.

Now, Claim 4 in our above proof of Lemma
9.3
can be rewritten
in a nice and compact form as the equality

\mathbf{A}\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{U}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\mathbf{A}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle\mathbf{A}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{A}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{A}$}}.

From this, we easily obtain

\mathbf{A}^{k}\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{U}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\mathbf{A}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle\mathbf{A}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{A}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{A}$}}^{k}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for any }k\in\mathbb{N}.

(43)

This equality essentially replaces Claim 3 in the above proof.

Setting
k=\rank u-\rank d
in (
43
), and comparing
the
\left(u,d^{\prime}\right)
-entries of both sides, we quickly obtain

A^{u\rightarrow d}=\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}^{u^{\prime}\rightarrow d^{\prime}}

(since
x\blacktriangleright d^{\prime}
holds only for
x=d
, and since

u\blacktriangleright x
holds only for
x=u^{\prime}
). This proves Lemma

9.3
again.

10
Proof of reciprocity: the case
j=1

Using the conversion lemma, we can now easily prove Theorem

4.6
in the case when
j=1
:

Lemma 10.1
.

Assume that
P
is the
p\times q
-rectangle
\left[p\right]\times\left[q\right]
. Let
i\in\left[p\right]
. Let

\ell\in\mathbb{N}
satisfy
\ell\geqslant i
. Let
f\in\mathbb{K}^{\widehat{P}}

be a
\mathbb{K}
-labeling such that
R^{\ell}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
. Let
a=f\left(0\right)
and
b=f\left(1\right)
. Then, using the notations from
Section
6
, we have

\left(i,1\right)_{\ell}=a\cdot\overline{\left(p+1-i,\ q\right)_{\ell-i}}\cdot b.

Proof 10.2
.

We have
\ell\geqslant i\geqslant 1
. Hence, Lemma
8.5
yields
that
\left(R^{\ell}f\right)\left(1,1\right)=a\cdot\overline{\left(R^{\ell-1}f\right)\left(p,q\right)}\cdot b
. In other words,
\left(1,1\right)_{\ell}=a\cdot\overline{\left(p,q\right)_{\ell-1}}\cdot b
.
This proves Lemma
10.1
in the case when
i=1
.

Hence, for the rest of this proof, we WLOG assume that
i\neq 1
. Thus,

i\geqslant 2
, so that
\ell\geqslant i\geqslant 2
, and therefore
R^{2}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
(by Lemma

3.23
, since
R^{\ell}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
). Hence, Lemma

3.29
yields that
a
and
b
are invertible (since
a=f\left(0\right)
and
b=f\left(1\right)
).

We have
\ell-i+1\geqslant 1
(since
\ell\geqslant i
) and
\ell-i+1\leqslant\ell
(since

i\geqslant 1
). The latter inequality entails
R^{\ell-i+1}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
(by Lemma

3.23
, since
R^{\ell}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
). Thus, Lemma

7.1

(b)
(applied to
\left(p-i+1,\ q\right)
and

\ell-i+1
instead of
v
and
\ell
) yields that the element
\left(p-i+1,\ q\right)_{\ell-i}
is well-defined and invertible.

Theorem
7.9

(d)
(applied to
\left(p-i+1,\ q\right)
and
\ell-i
instead of
u
and
\ell
) yields

\left(p-i+1,\ q\right)_{\ell-i}=A_{\ell-i}^{\left(p-i+1,\ q\right)\rightarrow\left(1,\ 1\right)}\cdot a.

Solving this for
A_{\ell-i}^{\left(p-i+1,\ q\right)\rightarrow\left(1,\ 1\right)}
, we obtain
A_{\ell-i}^{\left(p-i+1,\ q\right)\rightarrow\left(1,\ 1\right)}=\left(p-i+1,\ q\right)_{\ell-i}\cdot\overline{a}
, and thus

\overline{A_{\ell-i}^{\left(p-i+1,\ q\right)\rightarrow\left(1,\ 1\right)}}=\overline{\left(p-i+1,\ q\right)_{\ell-i}\cdot\overline{a}}=a\cdot\overline{\left(p-i+1,\ q\right)_{\ell-i}}.

(44)

For each
k\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,i-2\right\}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell-k}^{\left(p-k,\ q\right)\rightarrow\left(i-k,\ 1\right)}

\displaystyle=A_{\ell-k-1}^{\left(p-k,\ q\right)\rightarrow\left(i-k,\ 1\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{by (\ref{eq.prop.rect.trans.uv}), since we can easily}\\
\text{find }\ell-k\geqslant 2\geqslant 1\text{ and }R^{\ell-k}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell-k-1}^{\left(p-k-1,\ q\right)\rightarrow\left(i-k-1,\ 1\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{by Lemma \ref{lem.rect.conv}, applied to}\\
\left(p-k,\ q\right)\text{, }\left(p-k-1,\ q\right)\text{,}\\
\left(i-k,\ 1\right)\text{, }\left(i-k-1,\ 1\right)\\
\text{and }\ell-k-1\\
\text{instead of }u\text{, }u^{\prime}\text{, }d\text{, }d^{\prime}\text{ and
}\ell\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell-\left(k+1\right)}^{\left(p-\left(k+1\right),\ q\right)\rightarrow\left(i-\left(k+1\right),\ 1\right)}.

(52)

Now,

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{\left(p,\ q\right)\rightarrow\left(i,\ 1\right)}

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell-0}^{\left(p-0,\ q\right)\rightarrow\left(i-0,\ 1\right)}

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell-1}^{\left(p-1,\ q\right)\rightarrow\left(i-1,\ 1\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by
(\ref{pf.lem.rect.i1.short.2}), applied to }k=0\right)

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell-2}^{\left(p-2,\ q\right)\rightarrow\left(i-2,\ 1\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by
(\ref{pf.lem.rect.i1.short.2}), applied to }k=1\right)

\displaystyle=\cdots

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell-\left(i-1\right)}^{\left(p-\left(i-1\right),\ q\right)\rightarrow\left(i-\left(i-1\right),\ 1\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by (\ref{pf.lem.rect.i1.short.2}), applied
to }k=i-2\right)

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell-i+1}^{\left(p-i+1,\ q\right)\rightarrow\left(1,\ 1\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{since }p-\left(i-1\right)=p-i+1\\
\text{and }i-\left(i-1\right)=1\\
\text{and }\ell-\left(i-1\right)=\ell-i+1\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=A_{\ell-i}^{\left(p-i+1,\ q\right)\rightarrow\left(1,\ 1\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by
(\ref{eq.prop.rect.trans.uv})}\right).

(56)

However, Theorem
7.9

(c)
(applied to
u=\left(i,\ 1\right)
) yields

\displaystyle\left(i,\ 1\right)_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\overline{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{\left(p,\ q\right)\rightarrow\left(i,\ 1\right)}}\cdot b=\overline{A_{\ell-i}^{\left(p-i+1,\ q\right)\rightarrow\left(1,\ 1\right)}}\cdot b\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by
(\ref{pf.lem.rect.i1.short.3})}\right)

\displaystyle=a\cdot\overline{\left(p-i+1,\ q\right)_{\ell-i}}\cdot b\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by (\ref{pf.lem.rect.i1.short.1})}\right)

\displaystyle=a\cdot\overline{\left(p+1-i,\ q\right)_{\ell-i}}\cdot b.

This proves Lemma
10.1
.

In analogy to Lemma
10.1
, we have the following:

Lemma 10.3
.

Assume that
P
is the
p\times q
-rectangle
\left[p\right]\times\left[q\right]
. Let
j\in\left[q\right]
. Let

\ell\in\mathbb{N}
satisfy
\ell\geqslant j
. Let
f\in\mathbb{K}^{\widehat{P}}

be a
\mathbb{K}
-labeling such that
R^{\ell}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
. Let
a=f\left(0\right)
and
b=f\left(1\right)
. Then, using the notations from
Section
6
, we have

\left(1,j\right)_{\ell}=a\cdot\overline{\left(p,\ q+1-j\right)_{\ell-j}}\cdot b.

Proof 10.4
.

The two coordinates
u
and
v
of an element
\left(u,v\right)\in P
play
symmetric roles. Lemma
10.3
is just Lemma
10.1
with
the roles of these two coordinates interchanged. Thus, the proof of Lemma

10.3
is analogous to the proof of Lemma
10.1
.

11
Proof of reciprocity: the general case

Somewhat surprisingly, the general case of Theorem
4.6

follows by a fairly straightforward induction argument from Lemma

10.1
:

Proof 11.1

(Proof of Theorem
4.6
.)
.

We again use the notations from
Section
6
.

For any
\left(i,j\right)\in P
, we define
\tilt\left(i,j\right)
to
be the positive integer
i+2j
. Our goal is to prove
(
11
) for each
x=\left(i,j\right)\in P
and

\ell\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying
\ell-i-j+1\geqslant 0
and
R^{\ell}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
. We
will now prove this by strong induction on
\tilt x
.

Induction step:
Fix
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Assume (as the induction
hypothesis) that

(
11
) holds for each
x=\left(i,j\right)\in P
satisfying
\tilt x<N
and each
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying

\ell-i-j+1\geqslant 0
and
R^{\ell}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
.

We now fix an element
v=\left(i,j\right)\in P
satisfying
\tilt v=N
and
an
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying
\ell-i-j+1\geqslant 0
and
R^{\ell}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
.
Our goal is to prove that (
11
) holds for
x=v
.
In other words, our goal is to prove that
v_{\ell}=a\cdot\overline{v_{\ell-i-j+1}^{\sim}}\cdot b
.

We have
N=\tilt v=i+2j
(since
v=\left(i,j\right)
). We are in one of
the following six cases:

Case 1:
We have
i=1
.

Case 2:
We have
j=1
.

Case 3:
We have
j=2
and
1<i<p
.

Case 4:
We have
j=2
and
i=p>1
.

Case 5:
We have
j>2
and
1<i<p
.

Case 6:
We have
j>2
and
i=p>1
.

Let us first consider Case 1. In this case, we have
i=1
. Thus,
v=\left(i,j\right)=\left(1,j\right)
(since
i=1
). The definition of
v^{\sim}

thus yields
v^{\sim}=\left(p+1-1,\ q+1-j\right)=\left(p,\ q+1-j\right)
. Also,
\ell-\underbrace{i}_{=1}-j+1=\ell-1-j+1=\ell-j
, so that

\ell-j=\ell-i-j+1\geqslant 0
. In other words,
\ell\geqslant j
. Hence, Lemma

10.3
yields

\left(1,j\right)_{\ell}=a\cdot\overline{\left(p,\ q+1-j\right)_{\ell-j}}\cdot b.

In view of
v=\left(1,j\right)
and
v^{\sim}=\left(p,\ q+1-j\right)

and
\ell-i-j+1=\ell-j
, we can rewrite this as
v_{\ell}=a\cdot\overline{v_{\ell-i-j+1}^{\sim}}\cdot b
. Thus,
v_{\ell}=a\cdot\overline{v_{\ell-i-j+1}^{\sim}}\cdot b
is proved in Case 1.

Similarly (but using Lemma
10.1
instead of Lemma

10.3
), we can obtain the same result (viz.,
v_{\ell}=a\cdot\overline{v_{\ell-i-j+1}^{\sim}}\cdot b
) in Case 2.

Next, let us analyze the four remaining cases: Cases 3, 4, 5 and 6. The most
complex of these four cases is Case 5, so it is this case that we start with.

In this case, we have
j>2
and
1<i<p
. Recall that
v=\left(i,j\right)
.
Define the four further pairs

\displaystyle m

\displaystyle:=\left(i,j-1\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ u:=\left(i+1,j-1\right),

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle:=\left(i,j-2\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ t:=\left(i-1,j-1\right).

The conditions
j>2
and
1<i<p
entail that all these four pairs
m
,
u
,

s
and
t
belong to
\left[p\right]\times\left[q\right]=P
. Here is
how the five elements
v,m,u,s,t
of
P
are aligned on the Hasse diagram of

P
:

\textstyle{u\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{v\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{m\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{s}
\textstyle{t}
.

(57)

In particular, the two elements of
\widehat{P}
that cover
m
are
u
and

v
, whereas the two elements of
\widehat{P}
that are covered by
m
are
s

and
t
.

The map
P\rightarrow P,\ x\mapsto x^{\sim}
(which can be visualized as
“reflecting” each point in
P
around the
center of the rectangle
\left[p\right]\times\left[q\right]
)
“reverses” covering relations (i.e., if

x,y\in P
satisfy
x\gtrdot y
, then
x^{\sim}\lessdot y^{\sim}
). Hence,
applying this map to the diagram (
57
)
yields

\textstyle{t^{\sim}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{s^{\sim}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{m^{\sim}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{v^{\sim}}
\textstyle{u^{\sim}}
.

In particular, the two elements of
\widehat{P}
that are covered by
m^{\sim}
are
u^{\sim}
and
v^{\sim}
, whereas the two elements of
\widehat{P}

that cover
m^{\sim}
are
s^{\sim}
and
t^{\sim}
.

From
\ell-i-j+1\geqslant 0
, we obtain
\ell\geqslant\underbrace{i}_{>1}+\underbrace{j}_{>2}-1>1+2-1=2
, so that
\ell\geqslant 2
. Therefore,
\ell-1\in\mathbb{N}
and

2\leqslant\ell
.

Hence, from
R^{\ell}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
, we obtain
R^{2}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
(by Lemma

3.23
). Therefore, Lemma
3.29
yields that
a
and

b
are invertible (since
a=f\left(0\right)
and
b=f\left(1\right)
). Also, we have
R^{\ell-1}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
(since
R\left(R^{\ell-1}f\right)=R^{\ell}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}=R\left(\operatorname*{\bot}\right)
).

Set
k:=i+j-2
. Then,
k\geqslant 0
(since
i\geqslant 1
and
j\geqslant 1
), so that

k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Now, straightforward computations show that the four elements
m
,
u
,
s

and
t
of
P
satisfy

\tilt m<N,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \tilt u<N,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \tilt s<N,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \tilt t<N

(since
i+2j=N
). Hence, using the induction hypothesis, it is easy to see
that the five equalities

\displaystyle m_{\ell}

\displaystyle=a\cdot\overline{m_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}\cdot b,

(58)

\displaystyle s_{\ell-1}

\displaystyle=a\cdot\overline{s_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}\cdot b,

(59)

\displaystyle t_{\ell-1}

\displaystyle=a\cdot\overline{t_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}\cdot b,

(60)

\displaystyle m_{\ell-1}

\displaystyle=a\cdot\overline{m_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}}\cdot b,

(61)

\displaystyle u_{\ell}

\displaystyle=a\cdot\overline{u_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}}\cdot b

(62)

hold
24
24

24

In more detail: The induction hypothesis tells us that …

•

… we can apply (
11
) to
m
and
\left(i,j-1\right)
instead of
x
and
\left(i,j\right)
(since
m=\left(i,j-1\right)\in P
and
\tilt m<N
and
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and

\ell-i-\underbrace{\left(j-1\right)}_{\leqslant j}+1\geqslant\ell-i-j+1\geqslant 0
and

R^{\ell}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
). This yields (
58
) (since an
easy computation shows that
\ell-i-\left(j-1\right)+1=\ell-k
).

•

… we can apply (
11
) to
s
and
\left(i,j-2\right)
and
\ell-1
instead of
x
and
\left(i,j\right)
and

\ell
(the reader can easily verify that the requirements for this are
satisfied). This yields (
59
) (since an easy
computation shows that
\left(\ell-1\right)-i-\left(j-2\right)+1=\ell-k
).

•

… we can apply (
11
) to
t
and
\left(i-1,j-1\right)
and
\ell-1
instead of
x
and
\left(i,j\right)
and

\ell
. This yields (
60
).

•

… we can apply (
11
) to
m
and
\left(i,j-1\right)
and
\ell-1
instead of
x
and
\left(i,j\right)
and

\ell
. This yields (
61
).

•

… we can apply (
11
) to
u
and
\left(i+1,j-1\right)
instead of
x
and
\left(i,j\right)
. This yields
(
62
).

.

We have
\ell-1\in\mathbb{N}
and
R^{\ell}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
. Hence, the transition
equation (
13
) (applied to
m
and
\ell-1
instead of
v
and

\ell
) yields

m_{\ell}=\left(\sum\limits_{x\lessdot m}x_{\ell-1}\right)\cdot\overline{m_{\ell-1}}\cdot\overline{\sum\limits_{x\gtrdot m}\overline{x_{\ell}}}

(here we have renamed the summation indices
u
from (
13
) as
x
,
since the letter
u
is already being used for something else in our current
setting). Thus,

\displaystyle m_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(\sum\limits_{x\lessdot m}x_{\ell-1}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=s_{\ell-1}+t_{\ell-1}\\
\text{(since the two elements of
}\widehat{P}\text{ that}\\
\text{are covered by }m\text{ are }s\text{ and
}t\text{)}\end{subarray}}\cdot\,\overline{m_{\ell-1}}\,\cdot\underbrace{\overline{\sum\limits_{x\gtrdot m}\overline{x_{\ell}}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\overline{\overline{u_{\ell}}+\overline{v_{\ell}}}\\
\text{(since the two elements of }\widehat{P}\\
\text{that cover }m\text{ are }u\text{ and }v\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\left(s_{\ell-1}+t_{\ell-1}\right)\cdot\overline{m_{\ell-1}}\cdot\overline{\overline{u_{\ell}}+\overline{v_{\ell}}}.

(63)

On the other hand, from
k=i+j-2
, we obtain
\ell-k-1=\ell-i-j+1\geqslant 0
. Thus,

\ell-k-1\in\mathbb{N}
. Also,
\ell-\underbrace{k}_{\geqslant 0}\leqslant\ell
, so that

R^{\ell-k}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
(by Lemma
3.23
, since
R^{\ell}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
). Hence, the transition equation (
13
) (applied to

m^{\sim}
and
\ell-k-1
instead of
v
and
\ell
) yields

\displaystyle m_{\ell-k}^{\sim}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(\sum\limits_{x\lessdot m^{\sim}}x_{\ell-k-1}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=u_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}+v_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}\\
\text{(since the two elements of }\widehat{P}\text{ that}\\
\text{are
covered by }m^{\sim}\text{ are }u^{\sim}\text{ and }v^{\sim}\text{)}\end{subarray}}\cdot\,\overline{m_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}}\,\cdot\underbrace{\overline{\sum\limits_{x\gtrdot m^{\sim}}\overline{x_{\ell-k}}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\overline{\overline{s_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}+\overline{t_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}}\\
\text{(since the
two elements of }\widehat{P}\\
\text{that cover }m^{\sim}\text{ are }s^{\sim}\text{ and }t^{\sim}\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\left(u_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}+v_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}\right)\cdot\overline{m_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}}\cdot\overline{\overline{s_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}+\overline{t_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}}.

(64)

This entails that the elements
\overline{s_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}+\overline{t_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}
and
m_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}
of
\mathbb{K}
are invertible
(since their inverses appear on the right hand side of this equality). Hence,
their product
\left(\overline{s_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}+\overline{t_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}\right)\cdot m_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}
is invertible as well.

Also,
\ell-k\geqslant 1
(since
\ell-k-1\geqslant 0
) and
R^{\ell-k}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
. Hence,
Lemma
7.1

(a)
(applied to
\ell-k
and
m^{\sim}

instead of
\ell
and
v
) shows that
m_{\ell-k}^{\sim}
is well-defined and
invertible. Now, solving (
64
) for
u_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}+v_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}
, we obtain

u_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}+v_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}=m_{\ell-k}^{\sim}\cdot\left(\overline{s_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}+\overline{t_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}\right)\cdot m_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}.

This shows that
u_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}+v_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}
is invertible (since
the three factors
m_{\ell-k}^{\sim}
and
\overline{s_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}+\overline{t_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}
and
m_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}
on the right hand
side are invertible).

Taking reciprocals on both sides of (
64
), we
obtain

\displaystyle\overline{m_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}

\displaystyle=\overline{\left(u_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}+v_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}\right)\cdot\overline{m_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}}\cdot\overline{\overline{s_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}+\overline{t_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}}}

\displaystyle=\left(\overline{s_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}+\overline{t_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}\right)\cdot m_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}\cdot\overline{u_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}+v_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}}

(65)

(by Proposition
2.2

(c)
).

Comparing (
63
) with
(
58
), we obtain

\displaystyle a\cdot\overline{m_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}\cdot b

\displaystyle=\left(\underbrace{s_{\ell-1}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=a\cdot\overline{s_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}\cdot b\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.thm.rect.antip.short.4}))}\end{subarray}}+\underbrace{t_{\ell-1}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=a\cdot\overline{t_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}\cdot b\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.thm.rect.antip.short.4b}))}\end{subarray}}\right)\cdot\underbrace{\overline{m_{\ell-1}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\overline{a\cdot\overline{m_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}}\cdot b}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.thm.rect.antip.short.5}))}\end{subarray}}\cdot\,\overline{\overline{u_{\ell}}+\overline{v_{\ell}}}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(a\cdot\overline{s_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}\cdot b+a\cdot\overline{t_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}\cdot b\right)}_{=a\cdot\left(\overline{s_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}+\overline{t_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}\right)\cdot b}\cdot\underbrace{\overline{a\cdot\overline{m_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}}\cdot b}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\overline{b}\cdot m_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}\cdot\overline{a}\\
\text{(by Proposition \ref{prop.inverses.ab} {\boldmath(c)},}\\
\text{since
}a\text{ and }\overline{m_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}}\text{ and }b\text{ are
invertible)}\end{subarray}}\cdot\,\overline{\overline{u_{\ell}}+\overline{v_{\ell}}}

\displaystyle=a\cdot\left(\overline{s_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}+\overline{t_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}\right)\cdot\underbrace{b\cdot\overline{b}}_{=1}\cdot\,m_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}\cdot\overline{a}\cdot\overline{\overline{u_{\ell}}+\overline{v_{\ell}}}

\displaystyle=a\cdot\left(\overline{s_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}+\overline{t_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}\right)\cdot m_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}\cdot\overline{a}\cdot\overline{\overline{u_{\ell}}+\overline{v_{\ell}}}.

Multiplying both sides of this equality by
\overline{a}
on the left and by

\overline{b}
on the right (this is allowed, since
a
and
b
are
invertible), we obtain

\displaystyle\overline{m_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}

\displaystyle=\left(\overline{s_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}+\overline{t_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}\right)\cdot m_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}\cdot\underbrace{\overline{a}\cdot\overline{\overline{u_{\ell}}+\overline{v_{\ell}}}\cdot\overline{b}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\overline{b\cdot\left(\overline{u_{\ell}}+\overline{v_{\ell}}\right)\cdot a}\\
\text{(by
Proposition \ref{prop.inverses.ab} {\boldmath(c)})}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\left(\overline{s_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}+\overline{t_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}\right)\cdot m_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}\cdot\overline{b\cdot\left(\overline{u_{\ell}}+\overline{v_{\ell}}\right)\cdot a}.

Comparing this with (
65
), we obtain

\left(\overline{s_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}+\overline{t_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}\right)\cdot m_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}\cdot\overline{b\cdot\left(\overline{u_{\ell}}+\overline{v_{\ell}}\right)\cdot a}=\left(\overline{s_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}+\overline{t_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}\right)\cdot m_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}\cdot\overline{u_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}+v_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}}.

Cancelling the
\left(\overline{s_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}+\overline{t_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}\right)\cdot m_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}
factors on the left of this
equality (this is allowed, since
\left(\overline{s_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}+\overline{t_{\ell-k}^{\sim}}\right)\cdot m_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}
is
invertible), we obtain

\overline{b\cdot\left(\overline{u_{\ell}}+\overline{v_{\ell}}\right)\cdot a}=\overline{u_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}+v_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}}.

Taking reciprocals on both sides, we find

b\cdot\left(\overline{u_{\ell}}+\overline{v_{\ell}}\right)\cdot a=u_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}+v_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}.

Expanding the left hand side by distributivity, we rewrite this as

b\cdot\overline{u_{\ell}}\cdot a+b\cdot\overline{v_{\ell}}\cdot a=u_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}+v_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}.

(66)

However, (
62
) yields

\overline{u_{\ell}}=\overline{a\cdot\overline{u_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}}\cdot b}=\overline{b}\cdot u_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}\cdot\overline{a}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by Proposition \ref{prop.inverses.ab}
{\boldmath(c)}}\right).

Multiplying both sides by
b
from the left and by
a
from the right, we can
transform this into

b\cdot\overline{u_{\ell}}\cdot a=u_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}.

Subtracting this equality from (
66
), we obtain

b\cdot\overline{v_{\ell}}\cdot a=v_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}.

(67)

This equality expresses
v_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}
as a product of three invertible
elements (namely,
b
,
\overline{v_{\ell}}
and
a
). Thus,
v_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}
is itself invertible.

Taking reciprocals on both sides of (
67
), we now
obtain
\overline{b\cdot\overline{v_{\ell}}\cdot a}=\overline{v_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}}
. Hence,

\overline{v_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}}=\overline{b\cdot\overline{v_{\ell}}\cdot a}=\overline{a}\cdot v_{\ell}\cdot\overline{b}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by Proposition \ref{prop.inverses.ab} {\boldmath(c)}}\right).

Solving this for
v_{\ell}
, we obtain

v_{\ell}=a\cdot\overline{v_{\ell-k-1}^{\sim}}\cdot b=a\cdot\overline{v_{\ell-i-j+1}^{\sim}}\cdot b\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }\ell-k-1=\ell-i-j+1\right).

Thus,
v_{\ell}=a\cdot\overline{v_{\ell-i-j+1}^{\sim}}\cdot b
is proved in
Case 5.

The arguments required to prove
v_{\ell}=a\cdot\overline{v_{\ell-i-j+1}^{\sim}}\cdot b
in the Cases 3, 4 and 6 are similar to the one we have used
in Case 5, but simpler:

•

In Case 3, we have
s\notin P
. The “neighborhood” of
m
thus looks as follows:

\textstyle{u\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{v\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{m\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{t}

(instead of looking as in (
57
)). This
necessitates some changes to the proof; in particular, all addends that
involve
s
or
s^{\sim}
in any way need to be removed, along with the
equality (
59
).

•

Case 6 is similar, but now we have
u\notin P
instead. (Subtraction is
no longer required in this case.)

•

In Case 4, we have both
s\notin P
and
u\notin P
.

Thus, we have proved the equality
v_{\ell}=a\cdot\overline{v_{\ell-i-j+1}^{\sim}}\cdot b
in all six Cases 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 and 6. Hence, this
equality always holds. In other words, (
11
)
holds for
x=v
. This completes the induction step. Thus,
(
11
) is proved by induction. In other words,
Theorem
4.6
is proven.

As we have already seen (in Section
5
), this entails that
Theorem
4.5
is proven as well.

12
The case of a semiring

An attentive reader may have noticed that nowhere in the definitions of

v
-toggles and birational rowmotion do any subtraction sign appear. This
means that all these definitions can be extended to the case when
\mathbb{K}

is not a ring but a
semiring
.

A
semiring
is a set
\mathbb{K}
equipped with a structure of an
abelian semigroup
\left(\mathbb{K},+\right)
and the structure of a (not
necessarily abelian) monoid
\left(\mathbb{K},\cdot,1\right)
such that
the distributive laws
\left(a+b\right)c=ac+bc
and
a\left(b+c\right)=ab+ac
are satisfied (where we use the shorthand notation
xy
for
x\cdot y
). Some standard concepts defined for rings can be straightforwardly
generalized to semirings; in particular, any nonempty finite family
\left(a_{i}\right)_{i\in I}
of elements of a semiring
\mathbb{K}
has a
well-defined sum
\sum\limits_{i\in I}a_{i}
. Definition
2.1
, too,
applies verbatim to the case when
\mathbb{K}
is a semiring instead of a
ring. Thus, the definition of a
v
-toggle (Definition
3.8
) and the
definition of birational rowmotion (Definition
3.16
) can be applied to
a semiring
\mathbb{K}
as well. We thus can wonder:

Question 12.1
.

Do twisted periodicity (Theorem
4.5
)
and reciprocity (Theorem
4.6
) still hold if
\mathbb{K}
is
not a ring but merely a semiring?

If we assume that
\mathbb{K}
is commutative, then the answer to this
question is positive, for fairly simple general reasons (see
[
GriRob16
, Remark
10]
). However, no such general reasoning helps for noncommutative

\mathbb{K}
. Indeed, there are subtraction-free identities involving inverses
that hold for all rings but fail for some semirings. One example is the
identity
a\cdot\overline{a+b}\cdot b=b\cdot\overline{a+b}\cdot a
from
Proposition
2.3

(a)
: David Speyer has constructed
an example of a semiring
\mathbb{K}
and two elements
a
and
b
of

\mathbb{K}
such that
a+b
is invertible (actually,
a+b=1
in his example),
but this identity does not hold. See
[
Speyer21
]
for details.

Of course, this does not mean that the answer to Question

12.1
is negative; we are, in fact, inclined to suspect
that the question has a positive answer. Our proofs of Lemma
10.1

and Lemma
10.3
apply in the semiring setting (i.e., when

\mathbb{K}
is a semiring rather than a ring) without any need for changes;
thus, Theorem
4.6
holds over any semiring
\mathbb{K}
at
least in the case when one of
i
and
j
is
1
. Unfortunately, subtraction
is used in the proof of Theorem
4.6
, and we have so far been
unable to excise it from the argument. (With a bit of thought, we can convince
ourselves that subtraction is actually unnecessary if
p=2
or
q=2
, so the
first interesting case is obtained for
P=\left[3\right]\times\left[3\right]
.)

13
Other posets: conjectures and results

We now proceed to discuss the behavior of
R
on some other families of posets

P
. We no longer use the notations introduced in Section
6
.

13.1
The
\Delta
and
\nabla
triangles

When
p=q
, the
p\times q
-rectangle
\left[p\right]\times\left[q\right]
becomes a square. By cutting this square in half along its
horizontal axis, we obtain two triangles:

Definition 13.1
.

Let
p
be a positive integer. Define two subsets

\Delta\left(p\right)
and
\nabla\left(p\right)
of the
p\times p
-rectangle
\left[p\right]\times\left[p\right]
by

\displaystyle\Delta\left(p\right)

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(i,k\right)\in\left[p\right]\times\left[p\right]\ \mid\ i+k>p+1\right\};

\displaystyle\nabla\left(p\right)

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(i,k\right)\in\left[p\right]\times\left[p\right]\ \mid\ i+k<p+1\right\}.

Each of these two subsets
\Delta\left(p\right)
and
\nabla\left(p\right)
inherits a poset structure from
\left[p\right]\times\left[p\right]
. In the following, we will consider
\Delta\left(p\right)
and

\nabla\left(p\right)
as posets using these structures.

The Hasse diagrams of these posets
\Delta\left(p\right)
and

\nabla\left(p\right)
look like triangles; if we draw
\left[p\right]\times\left[p\right]
as agreed in Convention
4.1
, then

\Delta\left(p\right)
is the “upper
half” of the square
\left[p\right]\times\left[p\right]
, whereas
\nabla\left(p\right)
is the “lower
half” of this square.

Example 13.2
.

Here is the Hasse diagram of the poset
\Delta\left(4\right)
:

\textstyle{\left(4,4\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left(4,3\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left(3,4\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left(4,2\right)}
\textstyle{\left(3,3\right)}
\textstyle{\left(2,4\right)}
.

Here, on the other hand, is the Hasse diagram of the poset
\nabla\left(4\right)
:

\textstyle{\left(3,1\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left(2,2\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left(1,3\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left(2,1\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left(1,2\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left(1,1\right)}
.

Note that
\Delta\left(p\right)=\varnothing
when
p=1
.

Computations with SageMath
[
S+09
]
for
p=3
have made us suspect a
periodicity-like phenomenon similar to Theorem
4.5
:

Conjecture 13.3

(periodicity conjecture for
\Delta
-triangle)
.

Let

p\geqslant 2
be an integer. Assume that
P
is the poset
\Delta\left(p\right)
. Let
f\in\mathbb{K}^{\widehat{P}}
be a
\mathbb{K}
-labeling such that

R^{p}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
. Let
a=f\left(0\right)
and
b=f\left(1\right)
.
Let
x\in\widehat{P}
. We define an element
x^{\prime}\in\widehat{P}
as follows:

•

If
x=0
or
x=1
, then we set
x^{\prime}:=x
.

•

Otherwise, we write
x
in the form
x=\left(i,j\right)
, and we set

x^{\prime}:=\left(j,i\right)
.

Then,
a
and
b
are invertible, and we have

\left(R^{p}f\right)\left(x\right)=a\overline{b}\cdot f\left(x^{\prime}\right)\cdot\overline{a}b.

Conjecture 13.4

(periodicity conjecture for
\nabla
-triangle)
.

The same
holds if
P=\nabla\left(p\right)
instead of
P=\Delta\left(p\right)
.

If true, these two conjectures would generalize
[
GriRob15
, Theorem 65]
,
where
\mathbb{K}
is commutative.

13.2
The “right half” triangle

We can also cut the square
\left[p\right]\times\left[p\right]
along
its vertical axis:

Definition 13.5
.

Let
p
be a positive integer. Define a subset

\operatorname*{Tria}\left(p\right)
of the
p\times p
-rectangle
\left[p\right]\times\left[p\right]
by

\operatorname*{Tria}\left(p\right):=\left\{\left(i,k\right)\in\left[p\right]\times\left[p\right]\ \mid\ i\leqslant k\right\}.

This subset
\operatorname*{Tria}\left(p\right)
inherits a poset
structure from
\left[p\right]\times\left[p\right]
.

The Hasse diagram of this poset
\operatorname*{Tria}\left(p\right)
has
the shape of a triangle; if we draw
\left[p\right]\times\left[p\right]
as agreed in Convention
4.1
, then
\operatorname*{Tria}\left(p\right)
is the “right half” of
the square
\left[p\right]\times\left[p\right]
.

Example 13.6
.

Here is the Hasse diagram of the poset
\operatorname*{Tria}\left(4\right)
:

\textstyle{\left(4,4\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left(3,4\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left(3,3\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left(2,4\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left(2,3\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left(1,4\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left(2,2\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left(1,3\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left(1,2\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left(1,1\right)}
.

The inequality
i\leqslant k
in Definition
13.5
could just as well be
replaced by the reverse inequality
i\geqslant k
; the resulting poset would be
isomorphic to
\operatorname*{Tria}\left(p\right)
. But we have to agree
on something.

Now, we again suspect a periodicity-like phenomenon:

Conjecture 13.7

(periodicity conjecture for “right half” triangle)
.

Let
p
be a positive integer. Assume that

P
is the poset
\operatorname*{Tria}\left(p\right)
. Let
f\in\mathbb{K}^{\widehat{P}}
be a
\mathbb{K}
-labeling such that
R^{2p}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
. Let
a=f\left(0\right)
and
b=f\left(1\right)
. Let

x\in\widehat{P}
. Then,
a
and
b
are invertible, and we have

\left(R^{2p}f\right)\left(x\right)=a\overline{b}\cdot f\left(x\right)\cdot\overline{a}b.

If true, this conjecture would generalize
[
GriRob15
, Theorem 58]
, where

\mathbb{K}
is commutative.

In a sense, we can “almost” prove
Conjecture
13.7
: Namely, the proof of its commutative case
(
[
GriRob15
, Theorem 58]
) given in
[
GriRob15
]
can be adapted to the
case of a general ring
\mathbb{K}
, as long as the number
2
is invertible
in
\mathbb{K}
. The latter condition has all the earmarks of a technical
assumption that should not matter for the validity of the result;
unfortunately, however, we are not aware of a rigorous argument that would
allow us to dispose of such an assumption in the noncommutative case.

13.3
Trapezoids

Nathan Williams’s conjecture
[
GriRob15
, Conjecture 75]
, too, seems to
extend to the noncommutative setting:

Conjecture 13.8

(periodicity conjecture for the trapezoid)
.

Let
p
be
an integer
>1
. Let
s\in\mathbb{N}
. Assume that
P
is the subposet

\left\{\left(i,k\right)\in\left[p\right]\times\left[p\right]\ \mid\ i+k>p+1\text{ and }i\leqslant k\text{ and }k\geqslant s\right\}

of
\left[p\right]\times\left[p\right]
. Let
f\in\mathbb{K}^{\widehat{P}}
be a
\mathbb{K}
-labeling such that
R^{p}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
. Let

a=f\left(0\right)
and
b=f\left(1\right)
. Let
x\in\widehat{P}
.
Then,
a
and
b
are invertible, and we have

\left(R^{p}f\right)\left(x\right)=a\overline{b}\cdot f\left(x\right)\cdot\overline{a}b.

Again, this has been verified using SageMath for certain values of
p
and
s

and some randomly chosen
\mathbb{K}
-labelings with
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{Q}^{3\times 3}
. Even for commutative
\mathbb{K}
, a proof is yet to
be found, although significant advances have been recently made (see

[
Johnso23
, Chapter 4]
25
25

25

See also
[
DWYWZ20
]
for a proof on the
level of order ideals.
).

13.4
Ill-behaved posets

The above results and conjectures may suggest that every finite poset
P
for
which birational rowmotion
R
has finite order when
\mathbb{K}
is
commutative must also satisfy a similar (if slightly more complicated)
property when
\mathbb{K}
is noncommutative. In particular, one might expect
that if some positive integer
m
satisfies
R^{m}=\operatorname*{id}
(as
rational maps) for all fields
\mathbb{K}
, then
R^{m}f=f
should also hold
for all noncommutative rings
\mathbb{K}
and all
\mathbb{K}
-labelings

f\in\mathbb{K}^{\widehat{P}}
that satisfy
f\left(0\right)=f\left(1\right)=1
(the latter condition ensures, e.g., that the
a\overline{b}

and
\overline{a}b
factors in Theorem
4.5
can be removed).
However, this expectation is foiled by the following example:

Example 13.9
.

Let
P
be the four-element poset
\left\{p,q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}\right\}
with order relation defined by setting

p<q_{i}
for each
i\in\left\{1,2,3\right\}
. This poset has Hasse
diagram

\textstyle{q_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{q_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{q_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{p}
.

It is known (see
[
GriRob16
, Example 18]
or
[
GriRob16
, Corollary
76]
) that the birational rowmotion
R
of this poset
P
satisfies

R^{6}=\operatorname*{id}
(as rational maps) if
\mathbb{K}
is a field. In
other words, if
\mathbb{K}
is a field, and if
f\in\mathbb{K}^{\widehat{P}}

is a
\mathbb{K}
-labeling such that
R^{6}f\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
, then
R^{6}f=f
. But
nothing like this holds when
\mathbb{K}
is a noncommutative ring. For
instance, if we let
\mathbb{K}
be the matrix ring
\mathbb{Q}^{2\times 2}
,
and if we define a
\mathbb{K}
-labeling
f\in\mathbb{K}^{\widehat{P}}
by

\displaystyle f\left(0\right)

\displaystyle=I_{2}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{the identity
matrix in }\mathbb{K}\right),

\displaystyle f\left(1\right)

\displaystyle=I_{2},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ f\left(p\right)=I_{2},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ f\left(q_{1}\right)=I_{2},

\displaystyle f\left(q_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[c]{cc}1&0\\
0&-1\end{array}\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ f\left(q_{3}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[c]{cc}1&1\\
0&1\end{array}\right),

then
R^{m}f
is distinct from
f
(and also distinct from
\operatorname*{\bot}
) for all
positive integers
m
.

(See the detailed version of this article for a proof.)

Example 13.10
.

Let
P
be the four-element poset
\left\{p_{1},p_{2},q_{1},q_{2}\right\}

with order relation defined by setting
p_{i}<q_{j}
for each
i,j
. It
follows from
[
GriRob16
, Proposition 74
(b)
and Proposition 61]

that the birational rowmotion
R
of this poset
P
satisfies
R^{6}=\operatorname*{id}
(as rational maps) if
\mathbb{K}
is a field. On the
other hand, if
\mathbb{K}
is the matrix ring
\mathbb{Q}^{2\times 2}
, then
we can easily find a
\mathbb{K}
-labeling
f
of
P
such that
R^{m}f\neq f

for all
1\leqslant m\leqslant 10\ 000
(and probably for all positive
m
, but we have
not verified this formally), despite
f\left(0\right)
and
f\left(1\right)
both being the identity matrix
I_{2}
.

14
A note on general posets

We finish with some curiosities. While Theorem
4.6
is
specific to rectangles, its
\left(i,j\right)=\left(1,1\right)
case
can be generalized to arbitrary finite posets
P
in the following form:

Proposition 14.1
.

Let
P
be any finite poset. Let
f\in\mathbb{K}^{\widehat{P}}
be a labeling of
P
such that
Rf\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
. Let
a=f\left(0\right)
and
b=f\left(1\right)
. Then,

b\cdot\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
u\gtrdot 0\end{subarray}}\overline{\left(Rf\right)\left(u\right)}\cdot a=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
u\lessdot 1\end{subarray}}f\left(u\right),

(68)

assuming that the inverses
\overline{\left(Rf\right)\left(u\right)}

on the left-hand side are well-defined.

Proof 14.2
.

Even though we are not requiring
P
to be a rectangle, we shall use some of
the notations introduced in Section
6
. Specifically, we
shall use the notation
x_{\ell}
defined in (
9
), the notion of a
“path”, and the notations
A_{\ell}^{v}
,
\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{v}
,
A_{\ell}^{\mathbf{p}}
,

\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{\mathbf{p}}
,
A_{\ell}^{u\rightarrow v}
and

\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{\ell}^{u\rightarrow v}
defined afterwards. Hence, the equality
(
68
) (which we must prove) can be rewritten as

b\cdot\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
u\gtrdot 0\end{subarray}}\overline{u_{1}}\cdot a=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
u\lessdot 1\end{subarray}}u_{0}

(69)

(since
u_{1}=\left(Rf\right)\left(u\right)
and
u_{0}=f\left(u\right)
).

We assume that the inverses
\overline{\left(Rf\right)\left(u\right)}

on the left-hand side of (
68
) are well-defined
(since the claim of Proposition
14.1
requires this). We
furthermore WLOG assume that
P\neq\varnothing
(since the claim is easily
checked otherwise). Using these two assumptions, it is not hard to show that
both
a
and
b
are invertible. (See the detailed version for a proof.)

In Remark
7.12
, we have observed that Corollary

7.8
, Proposition
7.3
and parts

(a)
and
(b)
of Theorem
7.9
hold for our
poset
P
(even though
P
is not necessarily a rectangle).

Now, Theorem
7.9

(a)
(applied to
\ell=1
) shows that
each
u\in P
satisfies

u_{1}=\overline{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{1}^{1\rightarrow u}}\cdot b

and thus

b\cdot\overline{u_{1}}=b\cdot\underbrace{\overline{\overline{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{1}^{1\rightarrow u}}\cdot b}}_{=\overline{b}\cdot\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{1}^{1\rightarrow u}}=\underbrace{b\cdot\overline{b}}_{=1}\cdot\,\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{1}^{1\rightarrow u}=\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{1}^{1\rightarrow u}.

This latter equality also holds for
u=1
(indeed, from
1_{1}=b
, we obtain

b\cdot\overline{1_{1}}=b\cdot\overline{b}=1
; but it is easy to prove that

\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{1}^{1\rightarrow 1}=1
as well, and thus we obtain
b\cdot\overline{1_{1}}=1=\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{1}^{1\rightarrow 1}
). Therefore, it holds for all

u\in P\cup\left\{1\right\}
. Hence, in particular, it holds for all

u\in\widehat{P}
satisfying
u\gtrdot 0
. Summing it over all such
u
, we
obtain

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
u\gtrdot 0\end{subarray}}b\cdot\overline{u_{1}}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
u\gtrdot 0\end{subarray}}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{1}^{1\rightarrow u}=\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{1}^{1\rightarrow 0}

(since (
21
) (applied to
\ell=1
and
s=1
and

t=0
) yields
\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{1}^{1\rightarrow 0}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
u\gtrdot 0\end{subarray}}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{1}^{1\rightarrow u}\underbrace{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{1}^{0}}_{=1}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
u\gtrdot 0\end{subarray}}\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{1}^{1\rightarrow u}
). Therefore,

b\cdot\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
u\gtrdot 0\end{subarray}}\overline{u_{1}}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
u\gtrdot 0\end{subarray}}b\cdot\overline{u_{1}}=\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle A$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}_{1}^{1\rightarrow 0}=A_{0}^{1\rightarrow 0}

(70)

(by Corollary
7.8
, applied to
\ell=1
and
u=1
and

v=0
). Hence,

\displaystyle b\cdot\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
u\gtrdot 0\end{subarray}}\overline{u_{1}}

\displaystyle=A_{0}^{1\rightarrow 0}=\underbrace{A_{0}^{1}}_{=1}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
1\gtrdot u\end{subarray}}A_{0}^{u\rightarrow 0}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{by (\ref{eq.prop.slacks.rec.down1}), applied to }\ell=0\\
\text{and }s=1\text{ and }t=0\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
1\gtrdot u\end{subarray}}A_{0}^{u\rightarrow 0}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
u\lessdot 1\end{subarray}}A_{0}^{u\rightarrow 0}.

Multiplying both sides of this equality by
a
on the right, we obtain

b\cdot\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
u\gtrdot 0\end{subarray}}\overline{u_{1}}\cdot a=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
u\lessdot 1\end{subarray}}A_{0}^{u\rightarrow 0}\cdot a.

(71)

However, Theorem
7.9

(b)
(applied to
\ell=0
) shows
that each
u\in P
satisfies

u_{0}=A_{0}^{u\rightarrow 0}\cdot a.

This equality also holds for
u=0
(since
0_{0}=a
equals

\underbrace{A_{0}^{0\rightarrow 0}}_{=1}\cdot\,a=a
). Thus, it holds
for all
u\in P\cup\left\{0\right\}
. In particular, it therefore holds
for all
u\in\widehat{P}
satisfying
u\lessdot 1
. Summing it over all such

u
, we obtain

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
u\lessdot 1\end{subarray}}u_{0}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
u\lessdot 1\end{subarray}}A_{0}^{u\rightarrow 0}\cdot a.

Comparing this with (
71
), we obtain

b\cdot\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
u\gtrdot 0\end{subarray}}\overline{u_{1}}\cdot a=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\widehat{P};\\
u\lessdot 1\end{subarray}}u_{0}.

This proves (
69
) and, with it, Proposition

14.1
.

Proposition 14.3
.

Let
P
be any finite poset. Let
f\in\mathbb{K}^{\widehat{P}}
be a labeling of
P
such that
Rf\neq\operatorname*{\bot}
and
f\left(0\right)=f\left(1\right)=1
. Then,

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u,v\in\widehat{P};\\
u\lessdot v\end{subarray}}\left(Rf\right)\left(u\right)\cdot\overline{\left(Rf\right)\left(v\right)}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u,v\in\widehat{P};\\
u\lessdot v\end{subarray}}f\left(u\right)\cdot\overline{f\left(v\right)},

assuming that the inverses
\overline{\left(Rf\right)\left(v\right)}

on the left-hand side are well-defined.

Proposition
14.3
is essentially saying that the sum

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}u,v\in\widehat{P};\\
u\lessdot v\end{subarray}}f\left(u\right)\cdot\overline{f\left(v\right)}
is an invariant under birational
rowmotion
R
when
f\left(0\right)=f\left(1\right)=1
. This is a
noncommutative analogue of the conservation of the “superpotential”
\mathcal{F}_{G}\left(X\right)
of an

R
-system (
[
GalPyl19
, Proposition 5.2]
). We do not know whether such
invariants exist in the general case.

Proof 14.4

(Proof of Proposition
14.3
.)
.

This follows by combining
Proposition
14.1
with Proposition
3.18
.
(This is elaborated in the detailed version.)
